Lightest Classic American Muscle Cars

"
Which american classic muscle car is lightest and has best
weight distrobution?
When looking at the classic golden era of American muscle (roughly 1964–
1973), traditional muscle cars were front-heavy straight-line cruisers,
meaning a true "50/50" weight distribution didn't really exist out of the box.
However, when comparing weights and balance across the iconic
platorms, a clear winner emerges.
The Winner: The First-Generation Ford Mustang (1965–1966
Small Block)
If you conﬁgure it with a lightweight engine option, the 1965–1966 Ford
Mustang takes the crown for being both the lightest and having the best
weight distribution of the classic heavy-hiters (Mustang, Camaro, Firebird,
Challenger, Charger, and Nova).
The Weight: Early hardtop and fastback Mustangs with a small-block
V8 (like the 260 or 289 cubic-inch Windsor) or an inline-six had a base
curb weight starting at roughly 2,550 to 2,800 lbs, making them
signiﬁcantly lighter than big-block Chevelles or Mopar B-bodies that
easily tipped the scales past 3,500 to 3,800 lbs.

The Weight Distribution: Out of the factory, a small-block '65
Mustang sat at roughly 54% front / 46% rear. While still front-biased,
this was about as balanced as classic front-engine American muscle
got. (By comparison, big-block variants or heavier competitors ofen
sat closer to 58%–60% front-heavy).
Honorable Mentions
The Chevrolet Nova (1968–1972): Speciﬁcally the lightweight X-body
platorms with a small-block V8 or straight-six. They were compact,
relatively light (around 2,900–3,100 lbs for V8 models), and handled
surprisingly well with minor suspension tuning.
AMC Javelin (1968–1974): While slightly heavier than the early
Mustang (hovering around 3,100+ lbs), AMC's pony car had a slightly
more balanced layout than some of its cross-town rivals, though parts
availability and overall numbers lag behind Ford and GM.
Why Classic Muscle Cars Struggled with Balance
To get beter weight distribution in a classic muscle car today, many
vintage road-racing and autocross builders use a few standard tricks:
1. Engine Choice: Swapping out a heavy cast-iron big-block for an
aluminum small-block (or modern LS engine).
2. Relocation: Moving the heavy lead-acid batery from under the hood
to the trunk.
3. Radiator Swap: Upgrading to an aluminum radiator to shed pounds
oﬀ the very front nose.
With these modiﬁcations, an early small-block Mustang can easily be
brought down to a remarkably nimble 52/48 or even 50/50 balance.

small v6 for fuel economy

Swapping to a forced-induction four-cylinder or small V6 is one of the
smartest ways to keep the classic muscle styling while drastically improving
handling, weight balance, and everyday fuel eﬃciency.
By removing a heavy cast-iron V8 from the front nose, you shave 100–200+
lbs directly oﬀ the front axle. This pushes the car's weight distribution
remarkably close to a true 50/50 split, while a twin-charged (supercharged
+ turbocharged) setup ensures you retain immediate throtle response and
high top-end power.
Here are the best engines for this swap, along with how to achieve the
twin-charged or forced-induction setup eﬀectively:
1. Best Inline-4 Options
Option A: Volvo 2.0L Drive-E (VEA T6 / T8 Engine)
Factory Setup: Twin-charged out of the factory (Roots-type
supercharger for low-RPM response + turbocharger for high-end
boost).
Power Output: ~300 to 316 hp / 295 lb-f torque.
Fuel Economy: Capable of 30+ MPG highway.
Why it ﬁts: It's already engineered from the factory as a reliable, high-
output twin-charged engine.
The Catch: Since it’s natively mounted transversely (front-wheel
drive/AWD), mounting it longitudinally for a classic rear-wheel-drive
muscle car requires custom motor mounts, custom bellhousing
adapter plates, and a standalone engine control unit (ECU).
Option B: Ford 2.3L EcoBoost I-4
Factory Setup: Single twin-scroll turbocharger (Factory RWD crate
engine).

Power Output: 310–330 hp / 350 lb-f torque.
Fuel Economy: ~28–32 MPG highway.
Why it ﬁts: Ford sells the 2.3L EcoBoost as a standalone "Control Pack"
crate engine speciﬁcally designed for classic car swaps. It mounts
longitudinally to standard Ford rear-wheel-drive manual (T-56/MT82)
or automatic transmissions.
Adding Twin-Charging: While standard single-turbo, you can add a
small Roots-style supercharger (or electric supercharger/booster)
upstream of the turbo to turn it into a custom twin-charged setup.
2. Best Small V6 Options
Option A: GM 3.6L Twin-Turbo V6 (LF3 / LF4)
Factory Setup: Twin-turbocharged (Cadillac CTS-VSport / ATS-V).
Power Output: 420–464 hp / 430–445 lb-f torque.
Fuel Economy: ~24–27 MPG highway.
Why it ﬁts: Extremely modern, compact, aluminum block. Designed
for rear-wheel drive platorms (GM Alpha chassis), making
transmission pairing straightorward.
Twin-Charging Mod: Converting this twin-turbo V6 to a twin-charged
layout requires deleting one turbo in favor of a positive-displacement
supercharger feeding the remaining large turbo.
Option B: Ford 2.7L or 3.5L EcoBoost V6
Factory Setup: Twin-turbocharged.
Power Output: 325–400+ hp / 400+ lb-f torque.
Fuel Economy: ~25+ MPG highway.
Why it ﬁts: Abundant availability, incredible low-end torque, and
strong afermarket support for RWD swaps into vintage Ford
platorms.

Key Swap Considerations & Challenges
The Twin-Charging Tuning Challenge
Running both a supercharger and a turbocharger requires precise bypass
valving so the supercharger doesn't restrict airﬂow once the turbo reaches
full boost. A afermarket standalone ECU (such as Haltech, FuelTech, or
Megasquirt) with custom tuning is essential to manage boost control, fuel
mapping, and variable valve timing.
Would you like to explore a speciﬁc donor chassis (e.g., Mustang, Nova, or
Duster) or dive deeper into the drivetrain/ECU requirements for a custom
twin-charged setup?

Building a lightweight, twin-charged inline-4, AWD classic muscle car is
an ambitious, incredible project. To pull oﬀ an AWD conversion without
turning the car into a lifed 4x4, the chosen platorm needs two main
physical characteristics:
1. A wide engine bay with removable/unboltable front crossmembers
(to ﬁt the front diﬀerential, axle shafs, and oil pan without slicing into
structural frame rails).
2. A unibody chassis or subframe layout that accepts afermarket
independent front suspension (IFS) and custom tunnel clearance for
the transfer case/front driveshaf.
Three ideal classic platorms excel for this exact build, ranked by
engineering feasibility and ﬁnal execution.
1. The Top Pick: 1965–1970 Ford Mustang (or Mercury Cougar)
The 1st Gen Mustang is widely considered the ultimate candidate for this
speciﬁc setup for several reasons:
Incredible Weight Balance Potential: Starting with a light unibody
(~2,600 lbs stock), replacing the heavy factory front suspension and
V8 with a lightweight aluminum inline-4 shifs the engine position
further back. With an AWD transfer case near the transmission, the car
achieves near 49/51 to 50/50 static weight distribution.
Massive Afermarket Subframe Support: Companies like Roadster
Shop, Art Morrison, and Detroit Speed engineer full perimeter or front
subframe chassis for 1st Gen Mustangs. These remove the stock shock
towers, providing open space to route the twin-charging plumbing
(supercharger intake + turbo piping + intercooler) and position front
axle shafs.
Drivetrain Synergy: Utilizing a Ford 2.3L EcoBoost block ﬁted with
custom twin-charging (or a custom VW/Audi EA888 AWD setup) aligns
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 6 of 425

naturally with the chassis.
2. The Builder's Favorite: 1968–1974 Chevrolet Nova (X-Body)
If a GM platorm is preferred, the 3rd Gen Nova is the premier choice over
the Camaro:
Bolt-On Front Subframe: Unlike many unibody cars of the era, the
Nova uses a front subframe that completely unbolts from the body
shell. This allows the builder to fabricate or weld a custom front
crossmember tailored for a front diﬀerential and drive axles without
altering the body.
Engine Bay Clearance: The Nova features a tall, boxy engine bay with
ample room to mount an inline-4 vertically (longitudinally) while
running a front diﬀerential underneath.
Weight & Stance: Lightweight (~2,800–2,900 lbs) with a long front
hood line, allowing the engine to be set back behind the front axle
centerline for optimal handling.
3. The Underdog Option: 1970–1976 Plymouth Duster / Dodge
Dart (Mopar A-Body)
For a distinct build, Chrysler’s A-body platorm oﬀers unique structural
advantages:
Factory Torsion Bar Design: Mopar A-bodies do not use traditional
top-heavy spring towers; their front suspension relies on lower torsion
bars. Clearing those out for a modern coilovers setup leaves an open
canvas for running front axle shafs.
Ultra-Lightweight: A 1970 Duster shell is among the lightest muscle
car bodies ever built (under 2,800 lbs with a light motor).
Packaging: The engine bay easily ﬁts a longitudinally mounted inline-
4, leaving signiﬁcant space for the supercharger, turbo, and
intercooler plumbing.

Recommended Drivetrain Architecture for the AWD Setup
To keep the stance low and aggressive (rather than looking like an SUV),
avoiding traditional truck transfer cases is key. Two primary drivetrain
pathways exist:
1. Custom Longitudinal RWD/AWD Transfer Setup:
Transmission/Transfer Case: Use a BMW xDrive (from a
330i/430i N20/B48) or Audi Torsen longitudinal manual/ZF 8-
speed automatic setup paired with an afermarket standalone
controller.
Front Axles: Custom double-wishbone front suspension using
custom-length CV axle shafs and slim front diﬀerential units
(e.g., Nissan GT-R or BMW xDrive front diﬀs).
2. Full Drivetrain Donor Swap (Floorpan & Chassis Integration):
Splicing the ﬂoorpan and drivetrain of an AWD donor (such as a
VW Golf R, Audi S3/A4 Quatro, or Mitsubishi Lancer Evolution)
directly into the classic body shell.
Next Steps & Discussion
To narrow down the blueprint further:
1. Do you lean toward Ford (Mustang), GM (Nova), or Mopar (Duster)
aesthetics?
2. Are you aiming to build the AWD system from individual custom
components (custom subframe + standalone transfer case), or by
adapting a modern donor car's complete AWD ﬂoorpan/drivetrain?
Which body is capable of more perfomance for lower fuel
consumption?

Now what engine should we use?
For a lightweight, fuel-eﬃcient 1965–1966 Mustang Fastback with AWD,
selecting the engine comes down to how you solve the twin-charging
challenge alongside a longitudinal AWD transmission layout.
Two distinct engineering approaches stand out as the top choices for this
swap:
Strategy 1: Factory Twin-Charged + Modern AWD
Engine: Volvo 2.0L Drive-E Twin-Charged (B4204T27 / T6 / T8
Engine)
If you want a true factory-designed twin-charged 4-cylinder, this engine
is the benchmark.
Factory Specs: ~316 hp / 295 lb-f torque stock (easily tuned to 400+
hp).
The Twin-Charged Magic: Features a Roots-type supercharger
engaged via an electromagnetic clutch for instant boost oﬀ the line,
seamlessly handing oﬀ to a large turbocharger once revs build.
Fuel Eﬃciency: Capable of 32–36+ MPG highway on modern engine
management.
The AWD Transmission Pairing: Pair it with an Audi Quatro (Torsen)
AWD manual/dual-clutch transmission or a BMW xDrive setup.
Why it works: Because the Volvo Drive-E engine shares bellhousing
bolt-patern geometry with older Volvo blocks (which have
afermarket adapters available for longitudinal RWD/AWD
transmissions), it can be mounted North-South (longitudinally) inside
the Mustang’s engine bay.

Strategy 2: Custom Twin-Charged Crate Build (Easiest
Integration)
Engine: Ford 2.3L EcoBoost I4
If you want maximum afermarket support, eﬀortless Ford-on-Ford chassis
ﬁting, and bulletproof reliability, this is the builder’s favorite path.
Factory Specs: 310–330 hp / 350 lb-f torque stock (capable of 450+
hp).
Fuel Eﬃciency: 28–32 MPG highway.
Converting to Twin-Charged: The EcoBoost is single twin-scroll turbo
from the factory. To turn it into a twin-charged system, builders mount
a small, belt-driven Eaton M62 or TVS supercharger on the cold side,
running through a bypass valve into the main turbocharger.
The AWD Transmission Pairing: Use a Ford Focus RS / Lincoln MKZ
AWD drivetrain architecture or adapt a Ford 10R80 / MT82 rear
transmission paired with an afermarket BorgWarner/Magna
transfer case.
Why it works: Ford Performance sells standalone wiring control
packages for the 2.3L EcoBoost, making ECU tuning and sensor
integration far simpler than adapting foreign factory ECUs.
Strategy 3: The JDM High-RPM AWD Standard
Engine: Honda 2.4L K24 (Custom Twin-Charged)
If high-RPM power curves (8,000+ RPM) and unmatched tuning potential
are the priority, the Honda K24 is an iconic choice.
Specs: Standard naturally aspirated block built with forged internals,
ﬁted with a twin-charge setup (Krafwerks/Jackson Racing
supercharger feeding a Garret G-Series turbo).
Power & Eﬃciency: 500+ HP capable, while yielding 30+ MPG on
light throtle.
The AWD Transmission Pairing: The afermarket oﬀers complete K-
to-AWD conversion kits (adapting BMW xDrive or Honda CR-

V/Element AWD transfer cases to longitudinal layouts).

For the ultimate blend of OEM twin-charging integration and fuel economy,
the Volvo 2.0L Drive-E is hard to beat. For a build with abundant
afermarket support and simpler AWD packaging, a Custom Twin-Charged
Ford 2.3L EcoBoost ﬁts the '65 Mustang Fastback seamlessly.

What about ew10j4s?
The Peugeot EW10J4S (the high-revving 2.0L 16V naturally aspirated
engine found in the 206 RC / GTi 180 and Citroën C4 VTS) is an exceptional
engineering candidate.
Building a twin-charged, AWD 1965–1966 Mustang Fastback with an
EW10J4S creates a unique blend of vintage American styling and high-
revving French rally/hot-hatch performance.
Evaluating the EW10J4S through the lens of weight, twin-charging
capability, AWD integration, and fuel eﬃciency reveals key technical
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 11 of 425

factors:
1. Weight & Balance: A Major Advantage
Lightweight Aluminum Construction: Unlike its cast-iron
predecessor (the XU10), the EW10 family uses an all-aluminum block
and head. The bare engine weighs only around 100–105 kg (~225–230
lbs).
Chassis Distribution: Dropping this lightweight aluminum 2.0L into a
~2,550 lb '65 Mustang Fastback bay removes over 250 lbs directly oﬀ
the front suspension compared to a traditional Ford 289 V8.
Front axle balance: This shifs the car’s static weight distribution very
close to 50/50 (or even slightly rear-biased depending on the AWD
transfer case placement).
2. High-Revving Head & Twin-Charging Potential
The EW10J4S is prized for its cylinder head design, developed with
assistance from Lotus Engineering:
Variable Valve Timing (VVT) & Flow: It features Continuously Variable
Valve Timing (VVT) on the intake side, massive intake ports, and hollow
sodium-ﬁlled valves.
Twin-Charging Synergy: Standard high-rpm NA engines suﬀer down
low when converted to big turbochargers. Adding a small Roots/TVS
supercharger (e.g., an Eaton M45/M62) to deliver immediate 0–3,500
RPM boost, then leting a mid-to-large turbo take over while the
engine revs out to its 7,300+ RPM redline, solves turbo lag entirely.
Forged Internals Required: In stock form, the EW10J4S runs a high
11.0:1 compression ratio. To handle forced induction, swapping to
lower-compression forged pistons (e.g., 8.5:1 to 9.5:1) and forged H-
beam rods is essential before boosting.
3. AWD & Longitudinal Mounting Engineering
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM

Since the EW10J4S was originally installed transversely (East-West) in
front-wheel-drive Peugeots/Citroëns:
1. RWD / Longitudinal Bellhousing Adapters:
To mount it longitudinally (North-South) in the Mustang, a
custom adapter plate is needed.
Custom adapter plates exist to bolt EW/XU engines to BMW 5-
speed/6-speed (Getrag/ZF) manuals or Mazda RX-5/Miata
gearboxes.
2. AWD Packaging:
The BMW xDrive Route: Mating the EW10J4S to a BMW 6-speed
manual/automatic from an xDrive 3-Series provides a ready-made
longitudinal AWD transmission with an atached front transfer
case.
The Audi Torsen Route: Using a longitudinal Audi Quatro 01E or
02X manual transmission allows the front drive axles to exit right
behind the engine block.
3. Oil Pan & Intake Alignment:
The EW10J4S factory intake manifold sticks out signiﬁcantly.
Rotating the engine North-South in a Mustang's wide engine bay
gives plenty of room, but a custom intake plenum will streamline
the twin-charger piping.
A custom shallow oil pan or dry-sump system will be required to
clear the Mustang's new front AWD diﬀerential and axle shafs.
4. Fuel Eﬃciency Potential
Light Throtle Eﬃciency: As a lightweight 2.0-liter 4-cylinder driving a
sleek Mustang Fastback shell, cruising at 65–70 mph on ﬂat highway
will easily net 30–34+ MPG.
Engine Management: Ditching the factory Magneti Marelli ECU for a
modern standalone ECU (such as Haltech, MaxxECU, or Ecumaster)
allows precise control of the VVT, electronic bypass valve for the
supercharger and boost mapping for maximum mpg on highway

10/19/26, 2:00 AM

supercharger, and boost mapping for maximum mpg on highway
cruise.
Verdict: Is the EW10J4S a Good Choice?
If you enjoy unique, European-inﬂuenced engineering, an EW10J4S twin-
charged AWD '65 Mustang Fastback would be a head-turning, agile, and
high-eﬃciency build.

Breakdown of Key Trade-Oﬀs
1. Weight & Front Chassis Impact
Winner: EW10J4S (~225 lbs)
Why: The EW10J4S is exceptionally light for a 2.0L 16V engine.
Removing ~250–300 lbs from the front nose of a '65 Mustang
compared to a V8 allows the AWD front suspension and custom
steering geometry to work with minimal front bias. The Honda K24 is
close behind, while the EcoBoost and Volvo carry ~70–80 lbs more in
block casting and factory forced-induction dress.
2. Twin-Charging & Valvetrain Performance
Winner: Volvo Drive-E (Factory) / EW10J4S (Custom High-RPM)
Volvo: It comes twin-charged out of the box with factory bypass
valves, electromagnetic clutch controls, and intercooler routing
mapped out by OEM engineers.
EW10J4S: Converting the EW10J4S requires afermarket forged
pistons/rods to lower the factory 11.0:1 compression ratio down to a
boost-friendly 8.5:1–9.5:1. However, once built, the Lotus-designed
head and sodium-ﬁlled valves breathe beter at high RPM (7,300+
RPM) than the Ford EcoBoost or Volvo, providing a screaming top-end
power curve combined with instant supercharged low-end torque.
3. AWD Packaging & Drivetrain Synergy
Winner: Ford 2.3L EcoBoost / Volvo Drive-E
Ford EcoBoost: Ford Performance sells Control Packs designed for
longitudinal RWD/AWD layouts, with easily accessible transmission bolt
paterns and front diﬀerential options.

EW10J4S: Because it was natively mounted transversely in FWD hot
hatches (like the 206 RC/GTi 180), turning it North-South for a classic
Mustang chassis requires a custom adapter plate to mate with an
AWD longitudinal transmission (such as a BMW xDrive or Audi Quatro
box) and a custom shallow oil pan to clear the front axle driveshafs.
4. Fuel Economy Eﬃciency
Winner: Volvo Drive-E / Peugeot EW10J4S
Why: Both 2.0L displace smaller volume than the 2.3L EcoBoost and
2.4L K24. Cruising oﬀ-boost on ﬂat highways at 2,000–2,200 RPM, the
EW10J4S with modern standalone ECU fuel mapping will easily
achieve 30–34 MPG, leveraging the Mustang Fastback’s light body
shell.
Summary Verdict
Choose the Ford 2.3L EcoBoost if you want the most straightorward
swap with oﬀ-the-shelf crate management and Ford-on-Ford brand
heritage.
Choose the Volvo Drive-E if you want a turnkey twin-charged setup
straight out of the factory with minimal forced-induction fabrication.
Choose the Peugeot EW10J4S if you want an ultra-lightweight, high-
revving, highly unique French/European motorsport centerpiece. It
requires more custom machining for the longitudinal AWD layout and
forged internals for high boost, but it delivers unmatched power-to-
weight balance and character in a '65 Fastback.

What about TU5?
Comparing the Peugeot TU5 (most famously the TU5JP4, a 1.6L naturally
aspirated 16-valve engine found across millions of Peugeot, Citroën, and
Saxo/206 models) to the EW10J4S and other powerplants reveals why it is
one of the most beloved budget tuning engines in Europe.
Evaluating the TU5 for a twin-charged, AWD 1965–1966 Mustang
Fastback build brings out distinct diﬀerences in displacement, weight, and
build culture:

1. Weight: The Ultimate Featherweight Advantage
TU5 (~190 lbs): The TU5 is legendary for being exceptionally light and
compact. Because it uses a small-displacement cast-iron block with an
aluminum head, it weighs roughly 30–40 lbs less than the all-aluminum
EW10J4S and over 100 lbs less than a Ford EcoBoost.
Chassis Impact: Dropping a TU5 into a '65 Mustang Fastback removes
immense weight from the front axle. When paired with an AWD
system, a featherlight 1.6L engine ensures the front end feels nimble,
eliminating heavy understeer and pushing weight distribution even
closer to a neutral balance.
2. The Mighty 1.6L Turbo & Twin-Charging Culture
Motorsport Heritage: In Europe (especially in rally and track day
culture), the TU5 block is a titan. While it started out low-power,
builders routinely slap high-pressure turbochargers or superchargers
onto built TU5 botom ends.
Handling Boost: Unlike the EW10J4S (which has an 11:1 compression
ratio requiring careful internal changes to run high boost), certain TU5
variants or built versions handle forced induction brilliantly once ﬁted
with forged rods and pistons. Designing a twin-charged setup (small
roots blower + large turbo) on a 1.6L gives you incredible low-end
spool while keeping top-end screaming power.

Displacement Eﬃciency: At only 1.6 liters, a TU5 cruising lightly on the

all the engine options discussed.
4. The Big Compromise: Low End & Packaging
Torque Deﬁciency Oﬀ-Boost: A 1.6-liter naturally aspirated engine
makes very litle torque under 3,000 RPM. While the supercharger
portion of your twin-charged setup is designed speciﬁcally to ﬁx this,
driving it oﬀ-boost will feel signiﬁcantly slower than a 2.0L or 2.3L
engine.
Transverse-to-Longitudinal Conversion: Just like the EW10J4S, the
TU5 was natively mounted transversely in hatchbacks (like the Peugeot
206, 306, and Citroën Saxo). You will still face the same fabrication
hurdles: custom bellhousing adapter plates (to mate to a longitudinal
AWD box like a BMW xDrive or Audi setup), a custom oil pan to clear
the front axle shafs, and custom engine management wiring.
Summary Verdict
Choose the TU5 if your primary goals are absolute maximum fuel
eﬃciency, the lightest possible front-end weight, and a high-
revving European rally-build underdog charm.
The Trade-oﬀ: It requires more custom fabrication to mount
longitudinally and will rely heavily on the supercharger side of your
twin-charged setup to move a Mustang chassis oﬀ the line smoothly.

Final Verdict & Recommendation for an Iranian Build
1. If your top priority is keeping build costs as LOW as possible:
Go with the Xantia 2.0L (XU10J4R) or a Peugeot XU7 16V (L4).

available local parts that any mechanic in Iran can service.

modern aluminum tech:
Look for a Mazda 3 (2.0L MZR) donor. Just keep in mind that the
initial purchase price and parts replacement costs will be
noticeably higher inside Iran.

What This Means on the Road
1. Acceleration & Torque (Advantage: Mazda MZR):
Because the Mazda engine has an extra 0.4 liters of displacement and
an advanced variable valve timing setup, it produces roughly 30 more
horsepower and noticeably more low-end torque than the TU5. In a
lightweight classic Mustang shell, the Mazda MZR will feel genuinely
quick, responsive, and eﬀortless during highway passing.
2. The TU5 Experience:
The TU5 is a fantastic, bulletproof engine, but at ~110 hp, a naturally
aspirated TU5 in a classic car will feel more like an economical daily
cruiser than a sports car. You would have to rev it hard to get it
moving quickly.
The Final Trade-Oﬀ: Power vs. Cost (In Iran)
While the Mazda 3 MZR is the clear winner in power, torque, and native
RWD transmission ﬁting, you have to weigh it against local availability:
Choose the Mazda MZR if you want superior power, beter
acceleration, and easy RWD manual transmission bolting, and you
are willing to pay a higher price for parts and maintenance in Iran.
Choose the Peugeot TU5 if your absolute top priority is dirt-cheap
parts availability, maximum fuel economy, and eﬀortless
maintenance anywhere in Iran, and you don't mind sacriﬁcing
acceleration.
Given your goal of building a fun, fuel-eﬃcient, lightweight car that
balances cost and performance, the Mazda 3 2.0L MZR is the
performance winner, provided you are comfortable with its higher local
parts pricing compared to Peugeot.

Paired with a mazda 3 5 speed auto
What if its fwd?
If you keep the entire setup Front-Wheel Drive (FWD) by using the engine
and the transverse 5-speed automatic transmission straight out of a Mazda
3, it changes your project completely.
While keeping it FWD drastically simpliﬁes sourcing donor parts (you can
pull the entire front subframe, engine, and transmission straight out of a
donor Mazda 3), it creates a massive structural challenge for a classic
American muscle car:
The Big Challenge: Converting a RWD Classic to FWD
1. The Classic Mustang is a Rear-Wheel Drive Unibody:
A 1965–1966 Mustang was engineered from the ground up for a
longitudinal engine and a rear axle. There is no front ﬁrewall
clearance, subframe, or steering rack layout designed to hold a
transverse (East-West) engine and diﬀerential package.
2. Extensive Custom Fabrication Required:
To make it work, you would have to cut out the entire front half of the
Mustang’s inner structure, fabricate a custom front subframe, and
mount the Mazda 3's front cradle, steering rack, and suspension
struts into the Mustang. In short, you are building a custom space-
frame front end.
3. Loss of "Muscle Car" Driving Dynamics:
A classic Mustang relies on classic rear-wheel-drive proportions and
i ht t

10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 30 of 425

weight transfer. Turning it into a front-wheel-drive car means the front
tires have to handle steering, braking, and engine power all at once,
leading to heavy torque steer.
If You Go FWD: Are There Beter Platorms?
If you prefer an easy FWD setup using a common donor car in Iran (like a
Peugeot or a front-wheel-drive sedan), trying to put it into a classic
Mustang body will cause a fabrication nightmare.
Instead, builders who want a cheap, high-MPG, FWD daily driver using local
Iranian engines typically stick to hatchbacks and sedans where the chassis
is already designed for it—such as building a sleeper Peugeot 206 or a
modiﬁed IKCO platorm.
Summary Verdict
Can you put a FWD Mazda 3 engine and auto transmission into a
classic Mustang? Technically anything is possible with custom metal
fabrication, but it is not recommended. It defeats the layout of the
car, requires cuting up the chassis, and is far more complicated and
expensive than a traditional RWD setup.
Best path: If you want a cheap, easy-to-build car in Iran, stick to a
naturally aspirated engine paired with a rear-wheel-drive (RWD)
transmission so it drops cleanly into the classic Mustang's tunnel
without major structural surgery.

especially within the realities of the Iranian market.
To hit all three goals—cheap build costs, high performance, and extreme
eﬃciency—without falling into the trap of expensive imported parts, you
need an engine that is lightweight, naturally aspirated (or simply

Here are the best classic-route engine and automatic transmission
combinations that ﬁt your exact criteria:
1. The Local Heavyweight Champion: Peugeot XU10J4R (2.0L
16V) or XU7 16V (L4)
The Setup: A 2.0L 16V Peugeot/Citroën engine mated to a durable,
accessible RWD automatic transmission setup (or a custom/adapted
longitudinal automatic box common in regional rear-wheel-drive
platorms).
Why it ﬁts the "Classic Route":
Cost: Sourcing an XU series block from a local donor (like a
Peugeot 405, Pars, or Xantia) is dirt cheap in Iran.
Performance: The 16-valve head breathes exceptionally well,
pushing ~135–150 hp naturally aspirated. In a lightweight classic
chassis, it moves with serious authority.
Eﬃciency: Paired with a properly geared automatic transmission
on the highway, it will cruise comfortably around 30 to 32 MPG,
keeping fuel bills low.
2. The Modern Eﬃciency Route: Mazda MZR 2.0L
The Setup: Mazda 3 2.0L MZR block paired with a longitudinal RWD
automatic transmission (sourced from a rear-wheel-drive donor like an
NC Miata platorm or compatible RWD Mazda/Ford application).
Why it ﬁts the "Classic Route":
Performance: With ~150 hp and advanced variable valve timing, it
delivers crisp, modern throtle response.

Eﬃciency: This is your MPG king. In a lightweight classic shell, it

The Catch: While parts are more expensive in Iran than Peugeot

Performance & MPG: It makes 200+ hp stock (and much more if

than sticking to Peugeot or local-market platorms.

Is fwd better or rwd for performance handling and mpg?
When comparing Front-Wheel Drive (FWD) and Rear-Wheel Drive (RWD)
across performance, handling, and fuel economy (MPG), each layout has
distinct architectural advantages.
The breakdown across all three metrics details how they perform:
1. Performance (Acceleration & Power Delivery)
Winner: Rear-Wheel Drive (RWD)
Why: When you accelerate, weight naturally transfers backward onto
the rear tires (squating the rear end).
RWD capitalizes on this by puting power right where that extra
weight is, maximizing traction. This is why high-horsepower sports
cars and muscle cars are almost exclusively RWD.
FWD suﬀers from weight transfer away from the driven wheels. As
power increases, FWD cars struggle with wheelspin and torque
steer (where the steering wheel violently yanks to one side under
hard acceleration).
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 36 of 425

The Exception: FWD is generally more eﬃcient at puting down power
in low-traction environments (like snow or heavy rain) because the
heavy engine sits directly over the drive wheels, but for pure
performance, RWD wins.
2. Handling & Driving Dynamics
Winner: Rear-Wheel Drive (RWD)
Why: RWD separates the steering job from the driving (power
delivery) job. The front tires only steer, and the rear tires only push.
This results in cleaner, more precise steering feel and a balanced,
neutral chassis setup (ofen achieving close to a 50/50 weight
distribution).
FWD Handling: FWD cars bundle steering, braking, and power delivery
all into the front tires. This inherently causes understeer (the car
wanting to push wide and plow straight when cornering hard at the
limit). While modern FWD cars handle brilliantly (like hot hatches), they
lack the dynamic balance and push-from-behind feel of RWD.
3. Fuel Economy (MPG)
Winner: Front-Wheel Drive (FWD)
Why:
Drivetrain Loss: FWD cars use a transverse (side-to-side) layout
where the engine connects directly to the transmission and
diﬀerential in one compact unit. Power travels in a short, straight
line to the wheels, resulting in very low mechanical power loss
(usually around 10–15%).
Weight: FWD setups are lighter and require fewer components
(no long drive shaf running down the center of the car, no heavy
rear diﬀerential housing). Less weight means the engine works
less, improving MPG.
RWD: RWD requires a transmission, a long driveshaf, and a heavy
rear diﬀerential. This extra mechanical friction and weight creates
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 37 of 425

rear diﬀerential. This extra mechanical friction and weight creates
higher drivetrain power loss (typically 15–20%), slightly hurting
fuel economy.
Summary Comparison Table
The Verdict for Your Project
If your core priorities are maximum performance and pure handling feel,
RWD is the deﬁnitive winner.
However, if your absolute highest priority is the highest possible MPG and
mechanical simplicity, FWD has the numerical advantage—though as
discussed earlier, forcing a FWD drivetrain into a classic RWD muscle car
shell destroys that simplicity through heavy custom fabrication. Sticking to
a lightweight RWD layout gives you the classic driving dynamics you want

Im in love with awd if we could only put it in equation
If you are completely in love with All-Wheel Drive (AWD), it changes the
entire character of the build. Adding AWD to a classic, lightweight RWD
muscle car shell gives you the ultimate traction weapon: blistering zero-to-
sixty launches, incredible all-weather grip, and the ability to put down
power cleanly out of tight corners without a hint of wheelspin.
However, bringing AWD into the equation on a budget-conscious, high-
MPG, high-performance build inside Iran introduces a unique engineering
puzzle.
Here is how you can practically solve the AWD equation using local or
accessible donor architecture:
The AWD Blueprint for a Classic Shell
To build an AWD classic car without spending a fortune on custom one-oﬀ
exotic parts, you need a donor platorm that natively packages an engine, a
longitudinal transmission, and an AWD transfer case/front diﬀerential
together.
Option 1: The Audi / Volkswagen Longitudinal Quatro Route
The Setup: An Audi longitudinal engine (like an older 1.8T or a 2.0L
block) mated to a classic Audi Quatro manual transmission (such as
the 01E or 02X boxes) with a front diﬀerential integrated into the
transaxle housing.
Why it works: Audi’s longitudinal Quatro system is legendary. The
transmission sends power both backward to the rear diﬀerential and
forward via an integrated driveshaf to a front diﬀerential.
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 39 of 425

The Reality in Iran: While Audi 80, 100, or A4 Quatro models are
occasionally found, they are rare and parts are pricier than
Peugeot/IKCO parts. However, it is a fully engineered, tried-and-true
layout for converting classic cars to AWD.
Option 2: The BMW xDrive / Subaru Impreza Route
The Setup: A compact, lightweight 4-cylinder engine mated to an
AWD gearbox (like a Subaru EJ-series AWD manual transmission or a
BMW xDrive setup).
Why it works: Subaru transmissions are designed entirely around a
longitudinal boxer engine with symmetrical AWD, making them
popular for custom conversions.
How AWD Aﬀects Your Core Goals
1. Performance & Handling (Massive Win):
With AWD in a lightweight classic body shell, your 0–60 MPH times
will drop dramatically. Instead of spinning tires oﬀ the line, the car will
squat and hook up instantly, turning it into a corner-carving monster.
2. Fuel Economy (Slight Penalty):
Remember earlier how FWD won on MPG because of lower drivetrain
loss? AWD is the opposite. Driving four wheels means turning extra
diﬀerentials, transfer cases, and a heavier driveline. You will take a 2
to 4 MPG hit on the highway compared to a pure RWD setup (e.g.,
dropping from ~34 MPG down to ~30 MPG).
3. Build Budget & Complexity (The Real Challenge):
This is where your budget constraint gets tested. An AWD conversion
requires:
Cuting out the transmission tunnel to clear the transfer case.
Fabricating a custom front subframe and steering rack to clear
the front axle shafs coming out of the diﬀerential.
Custom-length front and rear driveshafs.
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 40 of 425

The Verdict: Is AWD Worth It?
If you are willing to take on a more advanced fabrication challenge, AWD is
the ultimate performance upgrade. It completely transforms how a
lightweight classic car drives, giving you supercar-level grip and launch
control.
To keep it as budget-friendly and practical as possible, the secret is ﬁnding
a complete longitudinal engine + AWD transmission donor (such as an
Audi or Subaru setup) so you don't have to mix-and-match custom adapter
plates across diﬀerent manufacturer components.
Do you want to explore how a speciﬁc AWD donor layout (like Audi
Quatro) would ﬁt physically under the frame of a lightweight classic shell?
So in reality awd is out of equation
Unless the classic car is awd?
You hit the absolute nail on the head. In reality, unless the classic car was
already built as an AWD platorm from the factory, forcing AWD into a
traditional rear-wheel-drive vintage chassis is an engineering and
ﬁnancial labyrinth.
Here is why that realization saves you from a massive project trap:
Why Factory-AWD Classics Don't Really Exist
Classic muscle cars (like 1960s Mustangs, Camaros, or classic European
sedans) were built exclusively as front-engine, rear-wheel-drive (FR)
layouts. There is no such thing as an aﬀordable, factory-AWD classic car
from that era unless you are looking at rare, high-end exotics like an Audi
Sport Quatro or a vintage Jensen FF—both of which cost a fortune and
are practically non-existent in Iran.
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 41 of 425

What Happens If You Try to Retroﬁt AWD?
To make a classic car AWD, you have to completely gut and rebuild the
underpinnings:
1. The Floor Pan: You have to cut out the stock ﬂoor and transmission
tunnel to weld in a massive custom tunnel that can ﬁt a bulky transfer
case.
2. The Front Suspension: A classic car's front crossmember has a solid
steering link right where the front diﬀerential and axle shafs need to
go. You have to custom-fabricate an independent front suspension
(IFS) with custom axle cutouts.
3. The Budget Explosion: What started as a "cheap budget build"
quickly turns into a multi-thousand-dollar custom fabrication project
requiring specialized engineering, custom-length driveshafs, and
endless trial-and-error.
The Smart, Cost-Eﬀective Conclusion
By accepting that AWD is out of the equation, your project instantly
becomes realistic, highly aﬀordable, and mechanically sound:
Stick to Lightweight RWD: A classic RWD layout gives you the
timeless driving dynamics, easy maintenance, and straightorward
drivetrain installation you need.
Keep It Cheap and Eﬃcient: Pair a lightweight vintage shell with a
local, highly accessible engine (like a Peugeot XU10/XU7 or Mazda
MZR) and a simple RWD manual or automatic transmission.
The Result: You get a car that is cheap to build, easy to ﬁx in Iran,
incredibly light on its feet, achieves fantastic highway MPG, and is
actually ﬁnishable without draining your bank account or siting in a
fab shop for ﬁve years.
Are you leaning toward keeping it a lightweight RWD manual for maximum
driver control and eﬃciency, or would you still prefer an automatic

impossible, since MX-5s were never oﬃcially imported in any meaningful
numbers there. Relying on an imported specialty sports-car transmission

source or service, let's look at what is practical in the Iranian market while
keeping the performance and fuel eﬃciency goals intact.
The Realistic Alternatives for an Automatic RWD Build in Iran
1. The Local / Regional Truck Route (Ford-Based RWD
Automatics)
The Setup: The Mazda MZR 2.0L block shares its core bellhousing bolt
patern with the Ford Duratec / Ranger family.
The Gearbox: Older 4-speed or 5-speed automatic transmissions
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be

found in light RWD trucks (like Ford/Mazda pickups) or regional utility

2. The Universal Adaptation Route (The Smart Compromise)
If sourcing a direct-ﬁt RWD automatic transmission for a Mazda engine
proves too diﬃcult in Iran, builders facing this exact problem usually pivot
to a custom adapter plate.
How it works: You keep the Mazda MZR 2.0L engine for its power and
modern eﬃciency, but you use a custom-cut steel or aluminum
adapter plate to mate it to a hyper-common, easily serviceable RWD
automatic transmission that is already abundant locally or regionally
(such as a classic Toyota or robust Nissan/GM automatic box).
Why this works: Toyota and Nissan automatic gearboxes from older
rear-wheel-drive sedans and pickups are much easier to ﬁnd, service,
and repair in local Iranian workshops than rare Mazda sports car units.
What is the Best Move?
If sourcing a 6-speed automatic feels too risky or scarce, you have two
choices depending on how deep into custom fabrication you want to go:
1. If you want an automatic without hunting for rare imports: Go with
a local-market engine block (like the Peugeot XU10 2.0L) paired via
adaptation to a common regional RWD automatic box. You lose a bit
of the exotic Mazda tech, but parts availability becomes a 10/10.
2. If you stay with the Mazda MZR engine: Be prepared to source the
automatic transmission via international shipping or accept that you
will need a custom machine shop to build an adapter plate mating
your MZR block to a more common RWD automatic gearbox.

What if we go manual?
Switching to a manual transmission completely solves the availability and
sourcing problem. It is arguably the smartest pivot you can make for this
project, especially when building in Iran.
Switching to a manual transforms the dynamic across every single goal:
Why a Manual Transmission Wins for Your Build
1. Parts Abundance & Cost:
Manual transmissions are mechanically simple, incredibly robust, and
inﬁnitely easier to source locally or regionally than rare automatic
gearboxes. No complex valve bodies, no electronic transmission
control units (TCUs), and no custom ﬂuid pumps to worry about.
2. Maximum Eﬃciency (MPG):
Manuals have virtually zero parasitic power loss compared to torque-
converter automatics. Coupled with the lightweight classic shell and a
Mazda MZR engine, a manual overdrive transmission will easily
squeeze out 34 to 36+ MPG on the highway.
3. Pure Performance & Control:
In a lightweight classic car, a crisp manual shifer gives you total
control over the power band. You can keep the naturally aspirated
engine screaming right in its sweet spot, making the car feel twice as
fast as an automatic.
How to Execute the Mazda MZR + Manual Setup

The beauty of the Mazda MZR 2.0L engine is that its bellhousing bolt

If you plan on force-inducing (turbocharging) whichever engine you
choose, the entire equation shifs. Forced induction changes how
displacement and torque behave, making the choice much clearer based
on physics and your build goals.
If both are geting boosted, the deﬁnitive winner is the XU10 2.0L 16V
(XU10J4R).
Why the Boosted XU10 2.0L Beats the Boosted TU5
1. Displacement is King with a Turbo:
A turbo engine relies on exhaust gas volume to spool up quickly.
The XU10 gives you 2.0 liters of displacement compared to the
TU5's 1.6 liters.
This means a turbo on an XU10 will spool earlier, hit harder lower
in the RPM range, and deliver massive, eﬀortless mid-range
torque. The TU5 will feel laggier and will need to be wrung out at
high RPMs to make comparable power.
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 49 of 425

2. The "No Replacement for Displacement" Rule on Low Boost:
Because you want high performance combined with high MPG, a
larger engine running low boost is vastly more eﬃcient than a
small engine screaming under high boost to make the same
power.
On the highway, the XU10 can loaf along eﬀortlessly in a
lightweight shell, sipping fuel. When you step on it, the turbo
kicks in smoothly without straining the block.
3. Stronger Factory Foundations:
The XU-series cast-iron block is an absolute tank. It handles
increased cylinder pressures and boost much more forgivingly
out of the box compared to lighter-duty small-displacement
blocks, meaning it requires less modiﬁcation to handle a reliable
turbo setup.
The Summary Blueprint for Your Boosted Manual RWD Build
The Engine: Peugeot XU10 2.0L 16V (Naturally aspirated high-
compression block ready for a safe, low-pressure turbo setup).
The Induction: A modest turbocharger running on a well-tuned
management map (giving you instant spool, ferocious acceleration,
and incredible top-end pull).
The Transmission: A robust manual RWD gearbox via local adaptation.
The Body: A lightweight classic RWD shell.
If everything is geting a turbo, skip the TU5. Go straight for the XU10 2.0L
16V—it will give you the torque, the high-end performance, and the
eﬀortless highway cruising you are looking for while keeping parts
completely native and cheap in Iran.

table, the engineering requirements change dramatically.
While twin-charging sounds like the ultimate no-compromise performance
setup, it introduces a brutal conﬂict with your goal of low fuel
consumption and low build complexity.
Here is how twin-charging impacts your project, and how it reﬁnes the
engine choice:
The Reality of Twin-Charging on MPG and Eﬃciency
1. Parasitic Drag (The Supercharger Penalty):
A supercharger is belt-driven directly oﬀ the crankshaf. Even when
you are just cruising on the highway and don't need boost, the
supercharger is constantly spinning and consuming engine power.
This directly hurts your fuel economy (MPG).
2. System Complexity & Heat:
Running a roots or centrifugal supercharger feeding into a
turbocharger requires complex plumbing, bypass valves, intercoolers,
and precise ECU tuning to manage the transition point between the
supercharger and the turbo. In a budget build in Iran, sourcing reliable
parts and tuners who can map a twin-charged system without
blowing head gaskets is extremely diﬃcult.
3. The XU10 Factor:
The XU10 block is strong enough to handle it, but packaging a
supercharger and a turbocharger into a classic car’s engine bay
alongside a custom RWD transmission layout will turn your clean,
lightweight build into a heavy, cramped engineering nightmare.
How to Get Twin-Charged Performance Without Twin-
Charging Drag
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 51 of 425

If your goal is instant throtle response oﬀ the line (what a supercharger
does) + massive top-end power and eﬃciency on the highway (what a
turbo does), modern tuning has a much beter solution: A Modern
Variable-Geometry Turbo (VGT) or a Small, High-Eﬃciency Twin-Scroll
Turbo.
Why it beats twin-charging: A modern twin-scroll turbo or a ball-
bearing turbo on a 2.0L XU10 block will spool up almost instantly
(mimicking the low-end feel of a supercharger), but unlike a
supercharger, it creates zero parasitic drag while you are cruising on
the highway.
The MPG Winner: When you are just maintaining speed on the
highway, the turbo stays quiet, the engine runs eﬃciently like a normal
naturally aspirated 2.0L, and you preserve your 32–34+ MPG. When
you smash the throtle, it lights up immediately.
The Final Recommendation for Low Consumption & High
Performance
If you want the absolute best balance of low fuel consumption,
bulletproof reliability in Iran, low build cost, and explosive performance:
Skip the twin-charger. It kills your highway MPG with parasitic drag
and turns the engine bay into an overly complex puzzle.
Stick to the XU10 2.0L 16V with a single, highly eﬃcient, properly
sized turbo (or twin-scroll setup).
Why it works: You get the 2.0L displacement for great low-end
behavior, a turbo for high-end muscle-car power, an iron block that
handles boost easily, and a setup that sips fuel on the highway
because the turbo isn't dragging down the crankshaf when you're just
cruising.
Does a clean, highly eﬃcient single turbo 2.0L XU10 manual RWD setup
hit that sweet spot of high performance and low consumption for you?

So xu10 or tu5 with VGt or high efficenct small turbo
When you weigh VGT (Variable Geometry Turbocharging) versus a high-
eﬃciency small turbo on either the XU10 or TU5, the choice comes down
to mechanical realism in Iran versus ultimate performance optimization.
Here is how they stack up for your exact goals of low consumption, high
performance, and budget-friendly execution:
Phase 1: VGT vs. Small High-Eﬃciency Turbo
1. Variable Geometry Turbos (VGT)
How it works: Uses movable vanes to change exhaust gas angle,
giving you zero turbo lag down low and massive ﬂow up top.
The Reality Check: VGT units are standard on modern diesel engines.
Puting a VGT on a high-revving gasoline (petrol) engine like a TU5 or
XU10 is extremely diﬃcult because gasoline exhaust gas temperatures
(EGTs) are much hoter than diesel. The vanes warp or seize unless you
source a rare, expensive, water-cooled gasoline VGT (like those found
on modern Porsche 911 Turbos).
Verdict: Impractical and expensive for a budget build in Iran.
2. A Small, High-Eﬃciency Modern Turbo (The Winner)
How it works: A modern, ball-bearing, quick-spooling turbocharger
(such as a compact Garret GT-series or a modern OEM turbo pulled
from a small displacement turbo car) matched to a twin-scroll
manifold.
Why it wins: It gives you the best of both worlds. A small, modern
compressor wheel starts spinning almost oﬀ-idle (eliminating lag), but
because it's a standard wastegated turbo, it handles high petrol
exhaust temperatures reliably, costs a fraction of a VGT, and is easily
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 53 of 425

tunable by local performance shops.
Phase 2: XU10 vs. TU5 with a Small Modern Turbo
Now that a small, high-eﬃciency turbo is locked in as the induction
method, the engine choice depends on how you want the car to behave:
Option A: The TU5 (1.6L 16V) + Small Turbo
The Character: Highly responsive, high-revving litle screamer.
Fuel Consumption: Unbeatable. On the highway, a small-displacement
1.6L engine under light boost or vacuum sips minimal fuel, easily
neting 36+ MPG.
The Trade-oﬀ: To make big power, the turbo has to work harder and
the engine has to spin higher. It requires more boost pressure to
match the torque of a 2.0L.
Option B: The XU10 (2.0L 16V) + Small Turbo (The Ultimate
Sweet Spot)
The Character: Eﬀortless, punchy, muscle-car-like delivery.
Fuel Consumption: Still excellent (32–34 MPG) because the extra 0.4L
of displacement means the engine doesn't need high boost to move a
lightweight classic shell. It can cruise lazily on the highway.
Why it wins: With a small modern turbo on a 2.0L block, turbo lag is
virtually non-existent. You get instant, neck-snapping low-end torque
from the larger displacement, combined with a turbo that sings
happily on the top end without being pushed to its breaking point.
Plus, the XU10's cast-iron block handles the thermal load of a turbo
much more reliably over time.
The Final Blueprint
Engine: Peugeot XU10 2.0L 16V (XU10J4R)
Induction: A small, modern, high-eﬃciency turbo (with a twin-scroll
manifold for instant spool)

To answer your speciﬁc question—will a VVT-equipped TU5 make more
power per unit of fuel (eﬃciency) compared to an XU10?—let's break
down how they compare when you factor in forced induction and weight:
1. Eﬃciency & Fuel Consumption (MPG)
Winner: The VVT Small-Displacement Engine (TU5 variant / EC5)
Why: At highway cruising speeds, a lighter 1.6L engine with VVT can
adjust its valve overlap to minimize pumping losses. Combined with
the lighter engine weight, it will burn less fuel per mile than a larger
2.0L block. If absolute maximum highway MPG is your holy grail, the
VVT 1.6L wins on paper.
2. Power Production Under Boost
Winner: The XU10 2.0L
Why Displacement Wins: There is an old engineering saying: "There is
no replacement for displacement."
A 1.6L engine is working much harder to make power. To match
the output of a 2.0L, the 1.6L must run higher boost pressure, rely
harder on high RPMs, and manage much higher cylinder
temperatures.
Under a turbocharger, the larger 2.0L XU10 naturally ﬂows more
exhaust gas volume, meaning its turbo spooing is lazier, smoother,
and hits with a broader, more eﬀortless wave of torque right oﬀ
the line.
The Real-World Trade-Oﬀ for Your Project
While a VVT 1.6L engine sounds like the ultimate high-tech, high-eﬃciency
choice, you have to weigh two major catches for your speciﬁc build:
1. The "Boost + VVT" Tuning Headache:
Factory VVT systems are mapped for naturally aspirated eﬃciency.
When you strap a turbo onto a VVT engine, the stock ECU (or a basic
standalone) doesn't know how to handle cam phasing under positive
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 56 of 425

standalone) doesn t know how to handle cam phasing under positive
manifold pressure. If not tuned with absolute precision, you risk valve
overlap issues under boost that can kill your engine or cause severe
detonation. A non-VVT engine (like the standard XU10) is inﬁnitely
more forgiving and predictable to turbocharge.
2. The "Weight vs. Torque" Balance in a Classic Shell:
Classic RWD shells (like a vintage lightweight body) are already light.
The weight diﬀerence between a TU5 and an XU10 is roughly 20 to 25
kg. While the TU5 keeps the front end slightly lighter, the 2.0L XU10's
extra torque means you don't have to downshif or push the engine
hard to move the car. In everyday driving, an engine that doesn't have
to work hard ofen matches the real-world fuel economy of a smaller
engine that is constantly being squeezed for power.
The Final Verdict
Choose the VVT 1.6L if: Your absolute top priority is squeezing out
every single decimal point of fuel economy, you love high-revving
small engines, and you are ready to invest in advanced tuning to
manage VVT under boost.
Choose the XU10 2.0L if: You want a punchy, eﬀortless muscle-car
feel with instant torque, a bulletproof cast-iron block that handles a
small turbo reliably without complex cam-phasing maps, and a build
that is signiﬁcantly easier to tune and maintain locally.
Given that you want very high performance alongside low consumption,
the XU10 gives you the performance headroom without needing to push a
tiny block to its absolute limits. Which characteristic maters more to you:
the high-tech eﬃciency of the small VVT engine, or the eﬀortless boosted
torque of the 2.0L?

2. Choosing the Ideal Gear Ratio for Performance + MPG
The gear ratio determines how many times the driveshaf spins for every
single rotation of the rear wheels.
The Danger of Short Ratios (e.g., 4.10 or 3.90): These give you
incredible acceleration oﬀ the line, but your engine will scream at high
RPMs on the highway, burning excess fuel and killing your MPG goal.
The Danger of Too Tall Ratios (e.g., below 3.08): While great for
cruising, a turbo engine paired with too tall of a gear will suﬀer from
lag when you try to accelerate without downshifing.
The Sweet Spot: 3.46:1 to 3.70:1 Ratio
If you pull or source a Peugeot 505 diﬀerential, look for these speciﬁc
factory ratios (or conﬁgure your ring-and-pinion accordingly):
Ratio Range: 3.46 (Code P) to 3.70 (Code M).
Why this is the golden balance for your build:
1. Low-End Punch: The turbocharged XU10 engine produces thick
low-end torque. A 3.58 or 3.70 ﬁnal drive multiplies that torque
nicely, ensuring that when you step on the gas in a lightweight
shell, the car launches hard without feeling sluggish.
2. Highway Overdrive & High MPG: Paired with a 5-speed manual
transmission's tall overdrive top gear, a 3.46–3.70 ﬁnal drive keeps
your highway cruising RPM comfortably low (around 2,200–2,500
RPM at 110 km/h). This is where your fuel eﬃciency shines, neting
you those target highway MPGs.

Diﬀerential Housing: Peugeot 505 RWD live-axle diﬀerential (or a
robust regional Toyota-derived unit if custom-ﬁted).

So the gearbox of a pegout 206+ 6 speed wont be good?
No, a Peugeot 206 / 206+ manual gearbox will not work for this project,
and trying to use one would create a massive mechanical problem.
Here is why:
1. It is Built for Front-Wheel Drive (Transverse):
The manual gearboxes found in a Peugeot 206 or 206+ are designed
to sit sideways (transverse layout) bundled directly together with the
diﬀerential inside a single front-wheel-drive transaxle case.
2. They Cannot Turn Power 90 Degrees:
To put power to the rear wheels in an RWD layout, you need a
longitudinal transmission (where the output shaf points straight
back toward the rear axle, like a traditional truck or rear-drive sedan).
You cannot simply bolt a FWD transverse gearbox into a rear-wheel-
drive tunnel.
Why the Toyota W-Series or a Local Truck Gearbox is Beter for
a Budget Build:
True RWD Design: Gearboxes like the Toyota W55/W58 or regional
RWD truck transmissions are built natively for longitudinal layouts.
They accept a standard driveshaf straight out of the tail housing.
Parts and Cost Eﬃciency: While you do need a custom machine shop
adapter plate to mate a Toyota gearbox to a Peugeot XU10 block, it is a
well-documented, routine procedure for Iranian mechanics who build
performance cars. Trying to custom-fabricate a transverse FWD
transmission into a longitudinal RWD setup would cost a fortune and
destroy reliability.

How much lighter duty are we talking about will it make any
problems? Because the lighter the better
When people talk about a "lighter-duty" diﬀerential or axle assembly (like
stepping down from a heavy Cressida/truck unit to a lighter Corona or
compact passenger car setup), the concern is torque shock under boost,
not normal driving.
Evaluating how much lighter-duty they are and whether it will cause
problems reveals a clear boundary line for your build:
1. What does "lighter-duty" actually mean here?
Cressida Axle (Heavy-Duty): Built for mid-to-large executive sedans
carrying inline-6 engines. The ring gear is thicker, the axle shafs are
thicker, and the diﬀerential carrier is beeﬁer. It adds a bit more weight,
but it is practically indestructible.
Corona / Classic Compact RWD Axle (Lighter-Duty): Built for smaller,
lighter 4-cylinder passenger cars (weighing around 900–1,000 kg).
The physical size of the diﬀerential pumpkin and the ring-and-pinion
teeth are smaller to save weight and reduce rotational drag.
2. Will it cause problems for your Turbo XU10?
It depends entirely on how you drive, because a turbocharged 2.0L 16V
engine produces sudden, violent spikes in torque when the turbo spols.
Where a lighter Corona diﬀ will struggle: If you slam the throtle
open aggressively, drop the clutch at high RPMs, or run sticky semi-
slick rear tires, a lightweight passenger car diﬀ will experience high
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 72 of 425

shock loading. The teeth on the smaller ring-and-pinion gear can chip,
strip, or shater under sudden hard launches.
Where it will survive just ﬁne: If you use normal street tires (which
allow the tires to slip slightly rather than sending 100% of the shock
straight into the gears) and build your boost progressively rather than
abusing it with brutal drag-race clutch kicks, a lighter passenger car
axle will hold up ﬁne and reward you with beter weight distribution
and responsiveness.
The Final Verdict: Weight vs. Reliability
Since your mantra is "the lighter the beter" to keep the classic shell
nimble and eﬃcient:
1. If you want maximum safety and zero mechanical anxiety: Go with
the Cressida setup. It is slightly heavier, but it completely removes the
risk of blowing up your diﬀerential when you turn up the turbo boost.
2. If you want maximum lightness and care deeply about agility: Go
with the Corona / compact RWD passenger car axle, keeping in
mind that you should drive it with mechanical sympathy—enjoy the
roll-on power of the turbo on the highway and twisty roads, rather
than treating every traﬃc light like a drag strip start.

The Breakdown: 1.8L vs. 2.0L (and 16V vs. 8V)
1. Engine Size: 1.8L vs. 2.0L
Fuel Economy (Advantage: 1.8L):
When oﬀ-boost (city traﬃc or light highway cruising), the 1.8L draws
less fuel per combustion cycle than a 2.0L. Combined with the Toyota
Cressida's 5th-gear overdrive transmission and tall diﬀerential ratio, a
1.8L engine will yield the highest highway MPG of all options
discussed.
Boost Potential:
A 1.8L requires slightly more boost pressure to match the torque of a
2.0L. However, because it displacement is smaller, a small
turbocharger will pressurize the intake plenum even faster, giving you
sharp, instant throtle response.

2. Valve Conﬁguration: 16V vs. 8V
16V (DOHC / SOHC 16-Valve):
Higher air-ﬂow eﬃciency at medium to high RPMs. It breathes beter
under boost, produces cleaner high-RPM power, and maintains high
fuel eﬃciency during open-road cruising.
8V (SOHC 8-Valve):
Mechanically simpler, extremely cheap to buy, and easier to service.
However, 8-valve heads restrict airﬂow at high RPMs. While ﬁne for
low-end torque, an 8V engine chokes earlier under turbo boost
compared to a 16V design.

Why a 1st Gen Mustang Works Exceptionally Well
1. Ideal Physical Proportions & Weight
Lightweight Factory Chassis: A 1st Gen Mustang Coupe or Fastback
weighs roughly 1,100 kg to 1,250 kg in stock form—remarkably light
for a classic muscle car.
The Weight Distribution Miracle: Early Mustangs were designed
around small-block Ford V8s and iron inline-6s. Dropping an all-
aluminum/cast-iron 1.8L/2.0L Mitsubishi Galant 16V turbo engine into
that bay removes over 100 kg oﬀ the front axle.
The Result: The car loses its historical nose-heavy slugginess,
sharpening turn-in response and overall handling dynamics.
2. Endless Bay Space for Custom Turbo Piping
Because the engine bay of a 1st Gen Mustang was built to accommodate a
V8, puting a compact longitudinal 4-cylinder in it gives you massive
working clearance. Fabricating custom turbo headers, placing a front-
mount intercooler, routing charge pipes, and servicing the engine become
eﬀortless.
3. Straightorward Drivetrain Alignment

Transmission: The Toyota W-series 5-speed manual (from a
Cressida) is remarkably compact compared to old Ford Toploader or
C4 automatics. It ﬁts inside the Mustang's transmission tunnel with

The Avoid List (For This Speciﬁc Build)

Why avoid: Way too heavy, atrocious fuel economy, and gear

Why avoid: Transaxle engine packages require extreme,
unnecessary fabrication to convert into a longitudinal rear-wheel-
drive Mustang engine bay.

1. The Ultimate Performance Foundation

motorsport world. Built with a robust iron block and a high-ﬂowing 16-valve
DOHC head, it is world-renowned for handling high turbo boost pressures
eﬀortlessly on factory internals. It gives your Mustang true performance
pedigree.
2. Inﬁnite Support & Parts
Because the 4G63 powered generations of rally cars and performance
sedans globally, ﬁnding afermarket components, turbo manifolds,
gaskets, and performance tuning gear is easier than almost any other 4-
cylinder engine on the market.
3. The Dream Drivetrain Ecosystem
Mating a longitudinal 4G63 block to your Toyota Cressida W-series 5-
speed manual transmission and Cressida rear live axle is a well-
established, bulletproof swap formula used by tuners worldwide. The
engine bolts up cleanly to RWD transmission setups (especially if sourced
from RWD Galant/Starion variants or adapted using well-known adapter
plates), and the Cressida rear end will handle every ounce of boost you
throw at it.

Does it really make that much difference in mpg and perf?
No, you do not need to stress or hold up your build hunting for a rare 4G63.
The standard 16V Galant engine (like the 1.8L 4G67 or standard 2.0L
4G63 N/A) that is widely available to you will perform brilliantly and give
you 95% of the experience for a fraction of the cost and headache.
The practical diﬀerences come down to performance ceiling and real-
world MPG:
1. Performance: Does it really make that big of a diﬀerence?
For a High-Boost Drag/Track Monster (350+ HP): YES, a true
performance-spec 4G63 maters because it has thicker cylinder walls
and oil squirters designed for extreme boost.
For a Fast, Daily-Driven Street Mustang (200–250 HP): NO, virtually
zero practical diﬀerence.
In a light 1,100 kg 1st Gen Mustang, 200 to 220 horsepower from a
turbocharged standard Galant 16V will make the car feel incredibly
quick—faster than a stock classic V8 Mustang, with instant throtle
response.
Standard 16V Galant blocks are built with heavy-duty Mitsubishi
cast-iron construction anyway. They handle 8 to 12 PSI of boost all

2. Fuel Economy (MPG): Which one is actually beter?
Standard 1.8L / 2.0L 16V Galant: WINNER for MPG.
Standard Galant 16V engines have slightly higher natural
compression ratios, lower internal friction, and smaller ports
designed speciﬁcally for air velocity and fuel eﬃciency.
When cruising on the highway oﬀ-boost in 5th gear with your
Toyota Cressida transmission, a standard 1.8L or 2.0L 16V will sip
fuel signiﬁcantly beter than a heavily tuned, rich-running
performance 4G63.
Performance 4G63 Turbo: Built with lower compression and tuned
richer to prevent detonation under high boost, meaning it burns
slightly more fuel even during casual driving.

Final verdict 1.8 or 2.0?
The ﬁnal verdict for your turbocharged 1st Gen Mustang build comes down
to 2.0L 16V Galant as the primary choice, with 1.8L 16V as a completely
acceptable backup.
1st Choice: 2.0L 16V Galant (The Champion)
Why it wins: Low-end torque. A 1st Gen Mustang chassis weighs
around 1,100–1,200 kg. The extra 200cc of displacement gives you the
punchy oﬀ-boost torque needed to move that body smoothly away
from stoplights without feeling sluggish before the turbo spools.
Fuel Economy: Virtually identical to the 1.8L on the highway. When
cruising in 5th gear on your Cressida W-series overdrive setup, the
2.0L doesn't have to work as hard to push air through the Mustang’s
classic body proﬁle, resulting in identical real-world highway MPG.
Parts & Tuning: Local machine shops and parts suppliers have
extensive experience with 2.0L Mitsubishi dimensions and gaskets.
2nd Choice: 1.8L 16V Galant (The Runner-Up)
When to pick it: If you inspect local yards and the 1.8L 16V blocks are
in signiﬁcantly cleaner, lower-kilometer condition or much cheaper

Compared to a nimble 1st Generation Mustang, a Chevrolet Nova (GM
Iran) is noticeably heavier and bulkier. A stock Nova tips the scales around
1,350 to 1,450 kg, and its front end feels heavy and traditional.
However, when you look at it through the lens of your speciﬁc build plan,
the bulkiness actually shifs in two very diﬀerent directions:
1. The Weight Advantage Afer the Swap
The Factory Weight Problem: A stock Nova carried a heavy cast-iron
inline-6 or a bulky small-block V8 upfront, making it feel sluggish in
tight corners.
The 4-Cylinder Cure: By pulling out that massive iron V8 and dropping
in your lightweight 2.0L 16V Mitsubishi block along with an aluminum
Toyota W-series transmission, you will instantly shred 150 to 200+ kg
right oﬀ the nose.
The Result: While the outer sheet metal is still an American compact-
sedan size, the front-end weight distribution drops dramatically,
curing a lot of that heavy, understeering "bulky" feel.
2. But It Still Isn't a Mustang
Even with a lighter engine, a Nova is a boxier, wider, and visually larger car
than a 1st Gen Mustang.
A Mustang coupe or fastback has a much shorter, tighter footprint,
aggressive fastback proportions, and weighs about 100–150 kg less
to begin with than a Nova.
If your heart is set on a true sports-car stance that handles like a
lightweight predator, the Nova will always feel a bit more like a
traditional cruiser.

Which of those has coupe in iran
In the Iranian domestic market, both the Chevrolet Nova and the Buick
Skylark (Buick Iran) were oﬃcially produced by Pars Khodro primarily as
4-door sedans, as those were the conﬁgurations heavily ordered for
government and family use prior to the revolution.
However, looking at them through the lens of a restomod builder:
1. The 2-Door Reality: Factory-assembled 2-door coupe versions of
either the local Nova or the Buick Skylark are exceptionally rare in Iran.
Most surviving examples you will encounter locally will be 4-doors.
2. The "Coupe-Sedan" Illusion: This is why the Buick Skylark has a
massive advantage for your build. Because of its sweeping, fastback-
style C-pillar, long hood, and aggressive window lines, the 4-door
Buick pulls oﬀ a sleek, coupe-like visual stance much beter than the
boxy, upright 4-door Nova.
If you speciﬁcally want a true 2-door pillarless or hardtop coupe look in
Iran, you would have to hunt down an imported grey-market classic (like a
2-door Chevy Camaro or an imported 2-door Nova/Skylark). But if you are
using the readily available local market platorms, the 4-door Buick Skylark
gives you that aggressive, low-slung, menacing proﬁle while keeping 4-
seater practicality.
Would you prefer to hunt for a rare imported 2-door body, or work with the
aggressive lines of the local 4-door Buick Skylark fastback shell?

aluminum Toyota manual transmission.
Final Curb Weight: ~1,250 kg (approx. 2,750 lbs) — remarkably light
for an aggressive 4-door American chassis, achieving that target
50/50 balance with driver and fuel onboard.

Depending on your boost setings and turbo choice (using a reliable street
tune on standard or mildly upgraded internals):
Low Boost (~8 to 10 PSI): ~180 to 200 Horsepower
The Result: Eﬀortless daily driving, perfectly responsive oﬀ-boost
torque, and completely docile in city traﬃc.
Moderate Boost (~12 to 14 PSI - The Sweet Spot): ~220 to 250
Horsepower with ~270–300 Nm of torque.
The Result: In a light 1,250 kg chassis, this power-to-weight ratio
will make the car exceptionally fast—substantially quicker oﬀ the
line and through the gears than a stock, heavy V8 Skylark, with
zero lag once the turbo kicks.

Summary of the Driving Dynamics
You end up with a vehicle that delivers modern 4-cylinder highway

chassis, and the menacing, low-slung, aggressive 4-door fastback
presence of an Iranian-assembled Buick Skylark.
Is it viable?
Yes, it is entirely viable, but with one major caveat that you must plan for
from day one: fabrication complexity.
The concept—pairing a lightweight, eﬃcient modern engine and
transmission into a classic American body shell—is a well-established hot-
rodding formula practiced worldwide (ofen called a "Restomod" or "Engine
Swap"). Mechanically and mathematically, everything we have mapped out
works.
Here is an honest breakdown of why it is viable, along with the speciﬁc
hurdles you will face making it happen in Iran:

Why It Is Viable (The Pros)
1. Massive Engine Bay Working Space:
The Buick Skylark / Nova X-body engine bay was originally engineered
to swallow a bulky V8 block. Dropping a compact inline-4 2.0L
Mitsubishi engine into it leaves you with incredible working room. You
will have easy access for turbo plumbing, intercooler routing, wiring,
and routine maintenance.
2. Readily Available Donor Parts Locally:
Unlike trying to import rare Ford or imported JDM chassis parts, Buick
Skylark and Chevrolet Nova body panels, glass, trim, suspension
components, and steering racks are heavily understood by local
Iranian mechanics and can be sourced through domestic classic
networks.
3. The Drivetrain Synergy:
The Mitsubishi 4-cylinder to Toyota Cressida manual transmission
swap is a classic mechanical pairing. Custom adapter plates or
bellhousing modiﬁcations for this family of swaps are well-
documented by builders globally.
4. Unmatched Fuel Eﬃciency & Power Balance:
Achieving 30+ MPG highway cruising while keeping a menacing 1970s
American muscle exterior and a 220-HP turbo setup solves the exact
problem of high fuel costs and daily usability.
The Real-World Challenges (What You Must Prepare For)
While completely viable, this is not a "bolt-on" kit. It is a custom
engineering project that requires:
1. Custom Engine Mounts & Crossmember Modiﬁcation:
You will need a skilled fabricator in Iran to cut, reinforce, and weld
custom engine and transmission mounts to place the Mitsubishi block
and Toyota gearbox into the Buick subframe at the correct angle.

Is it viable? Yes.
If you have access to a clean Buick Skylark shell, a standard 2.0L 16V
Mitsubishi engine, and a reliable Toyota Cressida drivetrain layout—backed
by a fabricator who knows how to measure and weld custom mounts—you
will build one of the most unique, eﬃcient, and aggressively stylish sleeper
restomods in the country.
Are you ready to start hunting down the rolling Buick shell and the engine
donor to kick oﬀ the project?
How to change the parts for less challenges
To minimize custom fabrication headaches, alignment issues, and endless
project downtime, you need to turn as much of this swap into a
"systemized matching game" as possible. When you are building a custom
restomod, every custom-machined part is a potential point of failure or
delay.
Here is the strategic playbook to change and install these parts with the
absolute minimum number of challenges:
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 108 of 425

1. Source the Engine and Transmission Together (If Possible)
The Challenge: Trying to mate a Mitsubishi engine to a Toyota
transmission requires custom bellhousing adapter plates, precise
ﬂywheel spacing, and custom clutch disc splines.
The Low-Challenge Fix: Whenever possible, see if you can source a
drivetrain where the engine and transmission are already happy
partners, or use a setup that has an oﬀ-the-shelf adapter kit.
Alternative option: If a standard RWD Mitsubishi gearbox (like
from an older rear-wheel-drive Galant or Lancer pickup) is
available in Iran, use that instead of the Toyota Cressida setup. It
eliminates transmission-to-engine mating headaches entirely
because it is bolt-on to the Mitsubishi block.
2. Keep the Factory Buick Steering & Suspension Geometry
Untouched
The Challenge: Cuting up the front crossmember or messing with
steering shafs to clear an oil pan is a nightmare.
The Low-Challenge Fix: When dropping the 2.0L 16V Mitsubishi engine
into the Buick Skylark bay, design your custom engine mounts
around the factory steering linkage and oil pan clearance, rather
than altering the car's steering geometry.
Sit the engine in the bay, check where the stock steering
box/linkage runs, and fabricate the engine mounts
upward/rearward to clear it cleanly.
3. Use Oﬀ-the-Shelf Cooling and Plumbing Components
The Challenge: Custom-bending every single piece of radiator hosing
and intercooler piping from scratch leads to leaks and endless ﬁting
issues.
The Low-Challenge Fix:
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 109 of 425

Radiator: Measure the Buick core support, then go to a local parts
shop and look for a heavy-duty aluminum radiator from a modern
truck or SUV (like a Toyota Hilux or Hyundai/Kia commercial
vehicle) that has its inlet and outlet ports lined up with your
Mitsubishi engine's thermostat housing. Bolt universal electric
fans to it.
Intercooler Piping: Buy a standard universal aluminum intercooler
piping kit with silicone couplers and T-bolt clamps. You can cut
and position them easily with basic hand tools rather than having
to custom-weld entire pipe runs.
4. Solve the Driveshaf the Right Way
The Challenge: Geting a driveshaf wrong means terrifying high-
speed vibrations and destroyed transmission tail-shaf seals.
The Low-Challenge Fix: Do not try to hammer or weld a driveshaf in
your garage. Once your engine, transmission, and rear diﬀerential are
ﬁrmly bolted into the Buick chassis, measure the exact yoke-to-yoke
distance down to the millimeter. Take the front half of your
transmission slip-yoke and the rear half of your diﬀerential ﬂange to a
specialized local driveshaf balancing shop. Let them weld a custom-
length tube and high-speed balance the entire assembly in one go.
5. Keep the Wiring Simple (Standalone or Simpliﬁed Harness)
The Challenge: Merging an old 1970s Buick chassis fuse box with
modern computer-controlled electronic fuel injection (EFI).
The Low-Challenge Fix: Isolate the two systems completely.
Keep the Buick’s basic chassis wiring purely for lights, ignition
switch, and dashboard gauges.
Run a dedicated, standalone wiring harness for the Mitsubishi
engine management system (EMS) with its own separate fuse and
relay box directly to the batery. This prevents electrical gremlins
from crossing over between the vintage body and the modern
engine
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 110 of 425

engine.
The Golden Rule for Minimizing Challenges:
Measure three times, cut once, and rely on standard oﬀ-the-shelf hoses,
belts, and oﬀ-the-shelf service items.
If you use a common belt size, a common radiator hose curvature, and a
clean standalone engine harness, any local mechanic in Iran can service the
car later without needing custom blueprints.
How do you want to handle the fabrication side—do you have a trusted
local shop in mind that specializes in engine swaps, or will you be managing
the mounting process yourself?
Galant vs cresseda gearbox
Comparing a Galant rear-wheel-drive gearbox directly against a Toyota
Cressida (W-series) gearbox for this Buick Skylark restomod comes down
to a clear matchup between factory ﬁtment and unbreakable strength.
Both are longitudinal rear-wheel-drive manual transmissions, but they
behave very diﬀerently in a project of this scale:
1. The Galant RWD Gearbox (e.g., KM132 / older Mitsubishi RWD
transmissions)
The Fitment Advantage (Plug-and-Play): If you use a Mitsubishi
engine, a factory Mitsubishi RWD transmission bolts straight up to the
block with zero adapter plates, zero custom bellhousing
modiﬁcations, and perfect clutch alignment out of the box.
The Structural Reality in Iran: Older rear-wheel-drive Mitsubishi cars
and pickup variants (like the Lancer/Galant RWD platorms of the
70s/80s) were imported, but parts, synchros, and clean donor
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
