transmissions are geting scarcer to ﬁnd in good condition locally.
Strength & Capability: Perfectly ﬁne for naturally aspirated or low-
boost setups, but if you start turning up the boost on a 2.0L 16V turbo
pushing 220+ horsepower hard through a heavy American chassis,
older Mitsubishi light gearboxes can reach their thermal and torque
limits.
2. The Toyota Cressida Gearbox (W50 / W55 / W58 W-Series)
The Legendary Strength: The Toyota W-series is universally famous
for being virtually indestructible. It is an aluminum-cased, heavy-duty
box designed to handle high torque, aggressive clutch drops, and
heavy mileage without complaining.
The Local Availability Advantage: Toyota Cressidas were widely used
in Iran (ofen as reliable workhorses and taxis), meaning Cressida
transmission components, rebuild kits, clutch master cylinders, and
complete gearboxes are much easier to source, service, and maintain
locally than rare RWD Mitsubishi boxes.
The Overdrive Cruising Ratio: Designed for highway cruising with a
deep 5th-gear overdrive, making it the absolute key to achieving your
high highway MPG goals.
The Catch (The Only Downside): Because it is a Toyota transmission
mated to a Mitsubishi engine, you will need a custom bellhousing
adapter plate or a custom-machined adapter bellhousing to bolt the
W-series box up to the 4G block.

The Final Verdict
Go with the Toyota Cressida W-series box if you want ultimate long-
term reliability, high-boost durability, easy local servicing, and great
highway cruising RPMs. The one-time custom adapter plate
fabrication is well worth the payoﬀ.
Go with a Mitsubishi RWD gearbox only if you stumble upon a
pristine, complete RWD donor setup and want to completely skip
dealing with transmission adapter plates.
Given your goal of a bulletproof setup that can handle turbo torque and
highway miles reliably in Iran, the Toyota Cressida transmission remains
the champion of the two.

Looking at what is historically available or accessible in the Iranian classic
market, two legendary Toyota longitudinal RWD engine families stand out
as direct drop-in matches for a Cressida gearbox:
Option 1: The Toyota 1G-GTE (2.0L Inline-6 24V Twin-Turbo)
If you want to stay at a 2.0L displacement bracket like we planned, but
want an absolute legendary inline-6 factory-turbo engine that bolts directly
to a Cressida W-series transmission:
The Vibe: Silky smooth Toyota inline-6 power. It sounds incredible
under a long American hood and matches the period-correct 1980s
turbo era.
Power Output: Factory twin-turbo versions push roughly 210
horsepower out of the box, with massive headroom for tuning.
The Catch in Iran: While Cressidas were common, clean 1G-GTE
performance engines are rare and sought afer. Wiring a twin-turbo
24-valve Toyota engine into a Buick chassis requires a custom
standalone ECU (like a Speeduino or Link/Haltech if accessible).
Option 2: The Toyota 2JZ-GE / 2JZ-GTE (3.0L Inline-6) or 1JZ
The Legendary Route: The 1JZ or 2JZ family bolts straight up to the
W-series Cressida transmission.
The Pro: Unbreakable bulletproof reliability, massive local knowledge
base (everyone knows how to tune a JZ), and terrifying power
potential.
The Catch for Our Goal: A 3.0L iron-block 2JZ is heavier than a 4-
cylinder Mitsubishi block. While it ﬁts easily in a massive Buick engine
bay, it adds more weight back onto the front axle, making it harder to
hit that strict 50/50 weight distribution target compared to a light 4-
cylinder.

make it compete with a turbo Mitsubishi 4G63, you would have to

high-eﬃciency edge.

The Verdict: Is a Matching Toyota Engine Beter?
If your absolute priority is zero transmission adaptation headaches:
Using a Toyota inline-6 (like a 1G or 2JZ series) paired directly to your
Toyota Cressida transmission is a mechanic's dream. Everything bolts
together mechanically from the factory ﬂywheel to the diﬀerential.
If your absolute priority is strict 50/50 weight distribution and
nimble handling: The Mitsubishi 4-cylinder block is shorter and
signiﬁcantly lighter than a long Toyota inline-6, meaning less nose-
heavy understeer when carving corners in your Buick Skylark.
If you had to choose between the lighter, high-revving 4-cylinder setup or
the pure factory-bolt-on ease of a Toyota inline-6 engine, which direction
leans closer to how you want to build this car?
No its i4 or i5 and it should be cheap to find
Which one is more realistic
Between the options we just weighed, using a native, older longitudinal
Toyota 4-cylinder block (or sticking to our original Mitsubishi engine +
Toyota gearbox plan via an adapter) is inﬁnitely more realistic than trying
to force a front-wheel-drive local engine into a rear-wheel-drive layout.
Here is why:
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 116 of 425

1. Why local transverse engines (like the EF7 or TU5) are a
fabrication nightmare
Engines like the EF7-TC or Peugeot TU5 are engineered purely for
transverse (sideways) FWD layouts found in modern Samands and
206s.
To make a transverse block sit longitudinally (front-to-back) in a RWD
Buick chassis, you don't just need a bellhousing adapter plate—you
essentially have to custom-fabricate an entire custom oil pan, modify
the oil pump pickup, reroute coolant housings, and deal with
intake/exhaust clearance issues against the ﬁrewall and steering shaf.
For a budget build, it turns into a massive engineering sinkhole.
2. The Most Realistic Path: The Classic Toyota Block + Toyota
Cressida Gearbox
If you want the lowest stress, lowest fabrication cost, and high reliability:
The Setup: A legacy longitudinal Toyota 4-cylinder block mated
directly to your Toyota Cressida W-series transmission.
Why it’s realistic:
1. Zero Adapter Plates: Toyota blocks and Toyota W-series
gearboxes bolt together like factory lego pieces.
2. Parts Availability: Basic maintenance parts, gaskets, and clutches
for this layout are straightorward.

2. Use Slot-In Universal Motor Mounts
Do not custom-fab frame rails. The Buick Skylark engine bay is

Buick live axle right where it is.

new brackets at exact pinion angles, and risking alignment vibration.

have to do for the rear is hand a tape measure to a local driveshaf

Toyota/Buick ﬂanges.

packaged cleanly so you aren't ﬁghting complex factory body-control

Does buick axle cause bad mpg and heavy?
That is a sharp question. Let's look at the two things you asked about the
original Buick axle: weight and fuel economy (gearing).
1. Is the Buick Axle Heavy?
Yes, relatively. The factory rear axle in a classic GM X-body or A-body
(like a Buick Skylark) is a heavy live-steel housing containing an iron
diﬀerential carrier and steel axle shafs.
The Impact: While it adds a bit of unsprung weight compared to an
aluminum modern setup, it is bolted solidly to your rear suspension
and doesn't aﬀect your front-to-rear weight balance nearly as much as
a heavy V8 engine up front. Shedding 150+ kg oﬀ the nose by ditching
the V8 far outweighs the weight penalty of the rear axle.
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 130 of 425

2. Does it Cause Bad MPG? (The Gearing Problem)
This is where you have to be careful. The factory Buick rear axle was
engineered decades ago to work with a heavy gas-guzzling V8 and a 3-
speed automatic transmission.
The Stock Gear Ratio Catch: Stock GM rear ends from that era
usually came with relatively "short" (high numerical) gear ratios—like
2.56, 3.08, or even 3.36. They were designed to get a heavy car moving
quickly from a dead stop, which means your engine has to spin at
higher RPMs on the highway.
How that hurts MPG: If your diﬀerential gears are too short, your
lightweight 4-cylinder engine will be screaming at 3,000+ RPM while
cruising at 110 km/h in 5th gear, completely ruining your 33+ MPG
highway eﬃciency goal.
How to Fix the Buick Axle for High MPG
You do not need to throw the Buick axle away or swap it out. You can keep
the rugged original housing and ﬁx the gearing problem easily:
Change the Ring and Pinion (Diﬀerential Gears): Inside your stock
Buick diﬀerential housing, you can swap out the gears for a "taller"
(lower numerical) highway-cruising ratio—ideally somewhere in the
2.73 to 3.23 range (depending on your tire size and engine torque
curve).
The Result: This drops your highway cruising RPM down to a lazy,
eﬃcient 2,000–2,200 RPM in 5th-gear overdrive, leting your 4-
cylinder turbo sip fuel eﬀortlessly on long stretches of road while
keeping the bulletproof strength of the stock GM axle housing.
The Botom Line
The stock Buick axle won't ruin your weight distribution, but its stock
factory gears might hurt your highway MPG if you don't adjust them.
Keeping the factory axle housing and swapping to a highway-friendly ring
and pinion gear is the ultimate way to get the best of both worlds: zero

Here is why keeping the modiﬁed Buick axle wins over a Toyota axle swap:
1. Zero Custom Fabrication (No Cuting or Welding)

premature axle wear.

Stock GM rear axles (like the 7.5-inch or 8.5-inch GM corporate units

never break a properly geared GM axle. It is massively overbuilt for a
light 1,250 kg car, meaning it will last forever.

3. Inﬁnite Gear Ratio Options

3.08, or 3.23 ratio) to pair with your Toyota 5-speed overdrive transmission.
This ensures your engine loafs along at low RPMs on the highway, securing
that high fuel economy.

keeps your rear suspension geometry completely factory-correct, and
gives you a bulletproof drivetrain setup from end to end.

series 5-speed transmission via an adapter plate, and then run a custom
driveshaf straight back to your re-geared Buick rear axle, your entire
powertrain is locked in and ready to perform.
Here is why this speciﬁc pairing (Toyota Gearbox + Modiﬁed Buick
Diﬀerential) functions as a complete, harmonious system:
1. Driveshaf Connection is Simple

transmission tail-shaf on one end, and the universal joint (U-joint)
matching your Buick diﬀerential yoke on the other end, and builds a

custom-balanced steel driveshaf tube to bridge the exact gap. This is

2. The Gearing Synergy (Overdrive + Highway Gears)
The Toyota W-Series 5th Gear: This transmission has a deep overdrive
ratio (typically around 0.85:1 or lower depending on the exact
W55/W57/W58 variant).
The Re-geared Buick Axle: By pairing that transmission overdrive with
a tall highway gear inside your stock Buick rear end (like a 2.73 or 3.08
ratio), you create a setup where your engine barely breaks a sweat at
high speeds.
The Result: You get eﬀortless acceleration from the lower gears in the
Toyota box, and relaxed, ultra-eﬃcient cruising on the highway, hiting
your target MPG without straining the engine.
3. Reliability Under Power
Both components are heavily over-engineered. A Toyota W-series
gearbox handles 200–250 horsepower all day long without breaking a
sweat, and a GM live-axle rear end is built out of heavy iron. They will
easily handle everything your turbocharged 4-cylinder throws at them.
You have a complete, bulletproof mechanical roadmap: an aggressive 4-
door Buick fastback shell, a lightweight 16V turbo engine up front, a reliable
Toyota shifing platorm in the middle, and a zero-fabrication, properly
geared stock Buick axle in the back.
Are you ready to start hunting down a clean Buick Skylark shell and your
donor engine components to kick oﬀ the project?

The Reality Check on the Custom Driveshaf
It helps to look at what a "custom driveshaf" actually means in the real
world: It is not a DIY garage fabrication.
You never cut a pipe or weld it yourself. You literally walk into a local truck
or driveline repair shop, hand them two ﬂanges, and say: "I need a steel
tube cut to this exact length with these two yokes welded on, and please
high-speed balance it." They put it on a lathe, weld it in 15 minutes, and
hand you a ﬁnished, vibration-free part.
Compared to cuting frame rails, fabbing custom subframes, or modifying
steering columns, a driveshaf shop handling your tube is the absolute
easiest, lowest-stress "fabrication" step in any restomod project.

Whichever costs less and works better
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be

Page 137 of 425

When you weigh cost, reliability, and safety, having a local driveline shop
build a custom driveshaf from scratch is both cheaper and works
signiﬁcantly beter than trying to splice two old factory shafs together.
Here is why that path wins on both fronts:
1. Why it Works Beter (Safety & Balance)
High-Speed Balance is Everything: A driveshaf spins at the exact
same speed as your wheels—sometimes upwards of 4,000 to 5,000
RPM on the highway. If a cut-and-welded junkyard hybrid shaf is even
slightly out of balance, it will create violent vibrations that destroy your
transmission tail-bearing and shake the car apart at 100+ km/h.
New Tubing vs. Old Metal: A driveline shop uses fresh, straight,
seamless high-strength steel tubing and puts the ﬁnal assembly on a
specialized balancing machine to weight-correct it. It guarantees a
smooth, vibration-free ride up to top speed.
2. Why it Costs Less (Time & Labor)
No Salvage Hunting: Hunting down two diﬀerent factory driveshafs
(one Toyota, one GM), measuring them, cuting them down, and trying
to align them wastes time and money.
One-Stop Shop: You walk into a driveline shop with your two matching
ends (the Toyota transmission slip-yoke and the Buick diﬀerential
ﬂange), give them a tape-measure length, and they assemble the
whole thing with brand-new universal joints (U-joints) in one go.
Because they do it every day for trucks and industrial vehicles, the
labor cost is remarkably low.

Leting a professional shop spin up a fresh, balanced driveshaf using your
exact transmission and diﬀerential ends is the ultimate "low-cost high-

exact transmission and diﬀerential ends is the ultimate low cost, high

throtle position, vehicle speed, and RPM in real time. It transitions

The computer manages this dual nature without any driver intervention
through two automatic mechanisms:
1. The Computer Controls Variable Timing Automatically
(Seamless MIVEC)
If you use a MIVEC-equipped cylinder head, you don’t toggle a switch to
change valve behavior. The ECU looks at a 3D map every millisecond:
Cruising / Light Throtle: When you are lightly pressing the pedal on
the highway, the ECU keeps the valve timing optimized for low fuel
consumption and minimal pumping losses.
Aggressive Throtle / Acceleration: The instant you push the gas
pedal down further, the ECU instantly alters the oil control valve to
advance or change cam lif. The transition happens continuously and
smoothly in fractions of a second. You don't feel a sudden jerk; the
engine just instantly breathes deeper and pulls harder.
2. Automatic Load-Based Mapping (No Eco/Sport Butons)
A standalone ECU uses a Throtle Position Sensor (TPS) and a Manifold
Absolute Pressure (MAP) sensor to construct a uniﬁed 3D fuel and
ignition map:
The High-MPG Zone (Vacuum / Low Load): When you're cruising at
110 km/h in 5th gear, engine load is very low. The ECU automatically
references the "Economy" cells of its map—running lean air-fuel ratios
and advanced timing to milk every drop of fuel out of the tank for that
33+ MPG.
The Performance Zone (Boost / High Load): The second you press
the accelerator to pass someone or take oﬀ from a stoplight, engine
load shoots up and the turbo builds boost. The ECU instantly shifs to
its high-performance fueling and ignition curves, delivering full power.

The Driving Experience
You get into the car, put it in gear, and drive.
When you are commuting or cruising on the highway, it behaves like a
docile, ultra-eﬃcient daily driver sipping minimal fuel.
When you lean into the throtle or drop a gear, it automatically
transforms into a hard-charging, quick-accelerating sports sedan—all
managed silently under the hood by the computer.
Would you like to focus next on mapping out the exact parts list—ﬁnding
the right donor engine and the matching transmission adapter—to get this
build moving?
Middle ground of best of both worlds
To get that true "middle ground" where the car adapts to you invisibly—
giving you maximum eﬃciency when you are just rolling along, and
explosive acceleration the moment you press harder—you rely on engine
load thresholds inside a programmable standalone ECU.
You do not need a dual-map toggle switch on the dash, and you don’t
necessarily even need complex variable valve timing (MIVEC) hardware that
might be harder to source locally in Iran. You can achieve this seamlessly
through smart electronic engine management mapping.
Here is how the computer blends both worlds into a single, eﬀortless
driving experience:
1. The Accelerator Pedal is Your Volume Knob (TPS/MAP
Blending)
Instead of an "Eco" or "Sport" buton the ECU reads how fast and deep you
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 143 of 425

Instead of an Eco or Sport buton, the ECU reads how fast and deep you
move the throtle:
Shallow / Slow Pedal Input (The Eﬃciency Zone): If you press the
pedal down 15% smoothly, the ECU knows you are just cruising or
maintaining city speed. It leans out the air-fuel mixture, uses
conservative boost targets, and maximizes fuel economy.
Deep / Fast Pedal Input (The Performance Zone): If you stab the
pedal down quickly or past 60%, the ECU instantly overrides the
eﬃciency bias. It dumps fuel, aggressively advances or retards timing
for instantaneous power, and spools the turbocharger immediately.
2. Gear-Sensing and Load Logic (How it handles the
transmission)
Because your Toyota 5-speed manual puts you in direct physical control of
the gears, the standalone ECU calculates engine load (how hard the engine
is struggling to move the weight of the car) to decide how to behave:
In 5th Gear Overdrive (High Speed, Low Load): The engine is barely
working because of your tall rear axle gears. The ECU recognizes this
low-load state and locks into ultra-lean, high-eﬃciency cruise control
mapping to hit your 33+ MPG goal.
In 1st, 2nd, or 3rd Gear (Low Speed, High Acceleration Demand):
The engine load changes drastically. The ECU instantly references
high-output ignition and fuel curves. Combined with a properly sized,
quick-spooling turbo, the car drops its fuel-saving behavior the
second you step on it, rocketing to 100 km/h in that 6.5–7 second
window.
Why This is the Ultimate Sweet Spot
You never have to think about what mode the car is in.
Driving through traﬃc or cruising on the highway feels calm, relaxed,
and extremely fuel-eﬃcient.

Does any of these diffs support esp?
No, neither the stock Buick diﬀerential nor a Toyota live-axle diﬀerential
supports ESP (Electronic Stability Program) natively on their own.
Here is why, and how it impacts your restomod build:
1. Diﬀerentials Do Not Control ESP
A diﬀerential is purely a mechanical gearbox designed to split torque and
allow your wheels to spin at diﬀerent speeds when turning. It has no
electronics, computers, or brake actuators.
ESP (Electronic Stability Program) / Traction Control is an active safety
system handled entirely by:
Wheel speed sensors on each hub.
A steering angle sensor and a yaw/ G-force sensor.
An ABS/ESP hydraulic modulator pump that can independently brake
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 145 of 425

An ABS/ESP hydraulic modulator pump that can independently brake
individual wheels and cut engine power.
2. Can You Add ESP to This Restomod?
Because you are building a classic 1970s/1980s Buick Skylark body with a
custom mechanical layout (Mitsubishi engine + Toyota transmission + Buick
live axle), the car will not have factory ESP.
Adding a modern OEM-style ESP system to a classic restomod is generally
not done because it requires retroﬁting a modern multi-channel ABS/ESP
brake pump, wiring up custom wheel speed sensor tone rings to your
custom axle and front spindles, and calibrating a donor stability computer
to a vehicle weight and wheelbase it was never designed for.
How to Get Modern Stability Without ESP
Since your build relies on clean, mechanical engineering and a predictable
chassis, you don't actually need factory ESP to make the car safe and
drivable:
The 50/50 Balance: By removing the heavy V8 and dropping in a
lightweight 4-cylinder turbo, the car's natural weight distribution
becomes balanced. It won't suﬀer from the severe understeer that
heavy classic American cars are known for.
Limited-Slip Diﬀerential (LSD): While the diﬀerential won't have
electronic stability, you can install a mechanical limited-slip diﬀerential
(like a Eaton Truetrac or similar center carrier) inside your Buick axle
housing. This ensures both rear tires put power down evenly around
corners, preventing one-wheel-peel and giving you predictable,
planted traction in the wet or dry.
Your build relies on mechanical grip, a lightweight chassis, and smart ECU
engine mapping rather than computer-controlled stability intervention.

I dig the traction control
Does it have abs?
No, the classic Buick setup does not come with ABS, and neither does a
standard standalone traction control system on its own.
Here is how traction control and ABS interact, and whether you can add
Anti-lock Brakes to your restomod:
1. The Diﬀerence Between Traction Control and ABS
Traction Control (The Acceleration Safety Net): Prevents your wheels
from spinning when you step on the gas too hard (especially in the
rain or mid-corner under turbo boost). It cuts power or ignition.
ABS (The Braking Safety Net): Prevents your wheels from locking up
when you slam on the brakes hard, allowing you to maintain steering
control so you don't skid.
2. Can You Add ABS to This Restomod?
While it is technically possible, retroﬁting full modern ABS into a classic
restomod is extremely diﬃcult and rarely done.
Why: A true ABS system requires an electronic hydraulic control unit
(the pump that pulses the brake ﬂuid), wheel speed sensors on all four
custom wheel hubs, a dedicated ABS computer, and custom tone
rings machined onto your axles and spindles. Integrating all of that
into a 1970s chassis requires custom fabrication and complex wiring.
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 147 of 425

3. How to Get Modern Braking Safety Without ABS
Since retroﬁting true ABS is a massive headache, most restomod builders
rely on high-performance mechanical braking setups that compensate
for the lack of computer-assisted anti-lock brakes:
Proportioning Valve: By installing an adjustable brake bias
(proportioning) valve, you can manually balance front-to-rear brake
pressure so the rear tires never lock up before the front tires during
heavy braking.
Quality 4-Wheel Discs & Modern Tires: Upgrading to modern multi-
piston disc brakes and wrapping your wheels in high-grip modern
radial rubber provides vastly more stopping power and traction than
any 1970s factory system ever had, drastically reducing the chance of
accidental wheel lock-up in normal driving conditions.
You get modern-day traction control through your standalone setup to
keep the rear wheels planted when you accelerate, while relying on
upgraded 4-wheel disc brakes and proper brake bias to keep things safe
and controlled when you stop.

Why it's cheap & easy: You get modern phone connectivity,

Can i even do a keyless start
Yes, absolutely. You can easily add a modern keyless push-buton start
system to your restomod, and it is actually much simpler to install on a
classic car than on a modern vehicle.
Here is how it works and why it ﬁts your build:
1 Why it's Easy on a Classic Car
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 150 of 425

1. Why it s Easy on a Classic Car
Modern cars tie their push-buton start systems into complex factory
immobilizers, body control modules (BCMs), and factory computer
networks. If something glitches, the car won't start.
Your restomod, however, has a clean, straightorward electrical system. A
universal afermarket push-buton start kit (like those from brands such as
Advanced Keys, EasyGuard, or generic RFID/push-buton kits) operates on
a basic logic of three simple circuits:
ACC (Accessory): Powers your radio, dash lights, and windows.
IG (Ignition): Powers your standalone ECU, fuel pump, and ignition
coils.
Start (Crank): Triggers the starter motor solenoid.
2. How the System Works
RFID Proximity Key Fob: You keep a small fob in your pocket. When
you walk up to the car, it recognizes you. When you walk away, it
automatically locks the doors.
The Push-Buton: Instead of turning a physical key in the ignition
cylinder, you simply press the brake pedal, tap the illuminated start
buton once, and the kit sequences the ignition and cranks the starter
until the engine ﬁres up.
3. Cleaning Up the Steering Column
Adding a push-buton start pairs perfectly with swapping out the old
steering column. When you install a modern afermarket steering column
(like we talked about for safety), you can choose one without an ignition
key cylinder, giving your dashboard a sleek, modern, shaved look while
entirely eliminating the risk of a steering-column lock jamming up while
driving.
It’s a cheap, highly reliable electronics upgrade that gives the interior a

Electronic steering?
Yes, you can add electronic power steering, but you have to choose the
right type.
Because your classic Buick Skylark uses a traditional mechanical steering
box rather than a modern rack-and-pinion setup, a standard electric rack
won't bolt in. However, restomodders have two great ways to get modern,
adjustable power steering without hacking up the engine bay or dealing
with messy hydraulic ﬂuid lines.
Option 1: Electric Power Steering (EPS) Column Conversion
(The Best Choice)
Instead of modifying your steering box or running a power steering pump
oﬀ your Mitsubishi engine, you hide an electric motor inside the cabin
under your dashboard.
How it works: You install a specialized afermarket steering column (or
modify your existing one) that has an electric servo motor integrated
directly into the shaf—typically sourced from a modern compact car
or an afermarket kit (like those from EPAS Performance). A small,
hidden control box lets you adjust the steering eﬀort level with a dial
(from heavy/sporty to light/parking-lot easy).
Why it ﬁts your build:
It completely eliminates messy hydraulic power steering pumps,
brackets, and hoses under the hood, keeping your engine bay
clean.
It works independently of your engine, so it doesn't rob any
horsepower or fuel economy.
Option 2: Electric-Hydraulic Power Steering (EHPS) Pump
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM

p
If you prefer the natural feel of a traditional hydraulic steering box:
How it works: You keep a standard GM manual or hydraulic steering
box, but instead of running a belt-driven pump oﬀ your engine, you
use a standalone electric-hydraulic pump (commonly harvested from
modern cars like a Volvo, Mini Cooper, or Mazda 3).
Why it works: The electric pump can be mounted anywhere hidden
(like behind the front bumper or inner fender), and it only turns on and
draws electrical power when you actually turn the steering wheel.

experience sharp and direct.

And i want it to handle good but be really comfortable too
Balancing sharp handling with plush, luxury-car comfort is the holy grail of a
great restomod—especially on a 4-door cruiser like a Buick Skylark. You
want it to take corners ﬂat and feel conﬁdent, but you also want it to
absorb bumps smoothly so it rides like a modern daily driver rather than a
stiﬀ race car.
To achieve that "sports-luxury" ride quality, you can combine a few smart
suspension and chassis tweaks:
1. Modern Gas-Charged Shocks with Progressive Springs
The Setup: Instead of rock-hard performance suspension, use high-
quality modern dampers (like KYB Gas-a-Just or adjustable
Bonn/Bilstein shocks) paired with progressive-rate coil springs.
Why it works: Progressive springs have tighter coils at one end and
looser coils at the other. When you are just cruising straight, the sof
coils absorb small bumps and potholes for a plush, comfortable ride.
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 156 of 425

coils absorb small bumps and potholes for a plush, comfortable ride.
When you corner hard and the car leans, the stiﬀer coils engage to
keep the body ﬂat and stable.
2. Modern Rubber / Polyurethane Bushings
The Setup: Replace the old, dry, squeaky rubber control arm and sway
bar bushings with fresh ones. Use polyurethane for the sway bar ends
(to keep body roll tight in corners) and high-grade rubber for the
control arms (to isolate road vibration and keep the ride smooth and
quiet).
Why it works: Old rubber rots out and makes the car sloppy. Fresh
bushings eliminate unwanted play in the steering without transmiting
harsh vibrations up through the ﬂoorboards.
3. Upgraded Front and Rear Sway Bars
The Setup: Thick anti-roll (sway) bars front and rear.
Why it works: Sway bars let you run relatively sof, comfortable
springs for a smooth ride, but they automatically step in during a
corner to stop the heavy body roll of a classic car. It gives you ﬂat,
conﬁdent cornering without sacriﬁcing straight-line comfort.
4. High-Proﬁle Tires on Modern Wheels
The Setup: Avoid ultra-thin rubber and giant 20-inch wheels, which
ride like a brick. Instead, stick to a healthy sidewall proﬁle (like 16-inch
or 17-inch wheels wrapped in quality modern radial tires).
Why it works: The sidewall of the tire acts as your ﬁrst line of defense
against rough roads. More rubber means a smoother, quieter, more
comfortable ride.
The Final Driving Feel
With this setup, the car ﬂoats comfortably over rough city streets and
highway expansion joints, but the second you turn the steering wheel into a

completely ruins the project's goals:
If you went naturally aspirated, your 0–100 km/h time would drop into
the sluggish 10+ second range.
If you went with an automatic, you’d lose the crisp driver's
engagement and hit fuel economy harder.
By pairing a manual transmission with a smart standalone ECU 3D
map, you get the absolute best eﬃciency and performance without
adding mechanical complexity.
4. What Could Make It "Cheaper" (And Why It's a Trap)

I love auto but wouldnt it be super difficult
You are completely right—it would add a layer of heavy complication,
which is why sticking to the manual transmission is the smarter move for
this speciﬁc project.
Trying to put an automatic behind a longitudinal RWD-swapped Mitsubishi
4G turbo engine introduces a few major headaches:
1. The Sourcing and Adapter Nightmare
Because the Mitsubishi 4G engine layout was primarily designed for front-
wheel-drive economy cars or transverse layouts, ﬁnding an oﬀ-the-shelf
automatic transmission adapter kit to mate it to a traditional RWD
automatic (like a GM Turbo-Hydramatic or similar) requires specialty
custom parts, specialized ﬂexplates, and custom torque converters. It
turns a straightorward bolt-on job into a custom fabrication project.
2. The Turbo Lag Problem with Old Autos
Older traditional automatics use loose torque converters that sap power
and let engine RPMs lag behind. On a small-displacement turbo engine, if
you don't have enough load instantly, the turbo won't spool. You’d end up
with a car that feels sluggish and lazy from a dead stop until the boost
ﬁnally catches up.
3. Killing Your Fuel Economy
As mentioned before, traditional automatics lack a solid mechanical lock-
up in the higher gears, meaning continuous hydraulic slip. That kills your
chances of eﬀortlessly cruising on the highway at 33+ MPG.
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM

The Smart Play
Keeping it a 5-speed manual keeps your build:
Simple: Using standard adapter components.
Reliable: Fewer things to overheat or break.
Eﬃcient: Direct gear connection for maximum fuel economy.
Responsive: You dictate exactly when the turbo builds boost.
If you love driving automatic cars for daily comfort, it makes sense why
you'd think about it—but in a custom restomod like this, a manual
transmission saves you time, money, and a massive mechanical headache.

Will this car run forever?
Mechanically speaking? Yes, prety close.
While no car in the world literally runs forever, this speciﬁc build is about as
close to bulletproof and immortal as a restomod can get. It is engineered
speciﬁcally to outlast modern cars and traditional classics alike, and here is
why:
1. The Engine is an Unbreakable Tank
The Mitsubishi 4G-series engine architecture (especially blocks like the
4G63/4G64) is legendary worldwide for being massively over-engineered.
They use iron blocks with incredibly strong stock internals. When you run a
moderate, reliable tune rather than chasing insane, engine-shatering
horsepower, these engines routinely run hundreds of thousands of
kilometers with basic maintenance.
2. Parts are Everywhere (No "Parts Extinction")
One of the biggest killers of classic cars is that original factory parts stop
being manufactured, leaving you stranded.
Your engine and transmission parts are sourced from mass-produced,
globally common vehicles (Mitsubishi and Toyota).
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 167 of 425

In Iran speciﬁcally, parts, gaskets, sensors, and components for these
drivetrains are widely available and well-understood by mechanics.
You will never be stuck hunting down a rare, extinct piece of
unobtanium.
3. Modern Electronics Fix the Old Weaknesses
Old cars usually die or become unreliable because of failing carburetors,
old points-ignition systems, and britle, roted 1970s wiring harnesses that
cause electrical ﬁres. By stripping out the old electronics and replacing
them with a modern wiring harness and a standalone ECU, you eliminate
90% of the headaches that put classic cars out of commission.
4. Simple Mechanical Design = Easy to Repair
Modern cars are packed with fragile computer modules, electronic sensors
everywhere, and sealed components that require a dealership laptop just
to ﬁx. Your restomod is refreshingly mechanical. If a part does wear out 10
or 15 years down the road, any competent mechanic with standard tools
can easily unbolt it and replace it.
The Only "Mortal" Enemies of the Car
The mechanical drivetrain can practically outlive us all, but if there is
anything that can hurt the car over decades, it is two things:
1. Rust: If the body sheet metal isn't properly treated, sealed, and
protected, corrosion can eat away at the classic Buick frame over
many years. (A good undercoating and paint sealant solves this).
2. Neglect: Regular oil changes and basic upkeep are still required.
If you keep it clean from rust and change the ﬂuids, this car is built to be a
reliable, lifelong daily driver that you could literally hand down to your kids.

Will injectors help with performance or mog?
Upgrading your fuel injectors will not magically increase your MPG or
your raw performance on its own, but they play a critical role in unlocking
and supporting both.
Think of fuel injectors like water faucets: they don't create water (power or
eﬃciency); they just control how cleanly and precisely it is delivered. Here
is how they actually aﬀect your goals:
1. Do they help with MPG? (Fuel Economy)
Short Answer: No, bigger injectors won't give you beter fuel
economy. In fact, if they are too big, they can make it harder for the
standalone ECU to precisely meter tiny amounts of fuel when you are
siting in traﬃc or lightly cruising, which can actually hurt your fuel
economy.
The Real Hero for MPG: Your fuel atomization and ECU tune. Modern
injectors with a ﬁne multi-hole spray patern (like Bosch EV14 style)
help atomize the fuel beter than old stock injectors, ensuring a
cleaner burn. But to hit your 33+ MPG, the heavy lifing is done by your
standalone ECU's lean-cruise 3D mapping and your tall Toyota
overdrive gearing, not by puting massive injectors in.
What you need: You just need injectors sized correctly for your power
goal (around 550cc–650cc for a 220–230 HP daily driver). They will
supply exactly what the engine needs—no more, no less—keeping
your cruising eﬃciency pristine

your cruising eﬃciency pristine.
2. Do they help with Performance?
Short Answer: Yes, but only if your stock injectors are maxed out.
The Reality: The stock factory injectors on a standard Mitsubishi turbo
engine are usually designed to support stock horsepower limits
(around 190–200 HP). If you turn up the boost slightly, demand more
air, and force the engine to make 220–230 HP, stock injectors can run
out of ﬂow capacity ("max out" at 100% duty cycle).
The Risk: If stock injectors max out, they go lean under boost, which
causes dangerous engine knock (detonation) and can destroy pistons.
Upgrading to slightly higher-output, high-impedance injectors gives
you the headroom to run your target boost safely and reliably without
starving the engine of fuel when you accelerate hard.
The Botom Line
Don't oversize them looking for extra MPG or power—match them
precisely to your 220–230 HP target.
Do buy quality modern injectors (like matched high-impedance
Bosch-style units) so they spray a ﬁne, even mist, which gives you
smooth idling, clean emissions, safe boost under acceleration, and
reliable daily driving.
So only iridium plugs
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be

Yes, exactly—stick to quality, single-electrode Iridium plugs.
For a turbocharged engine, a standard single-electrode NGK Iridium (like
the NGK Iridium IX series) is the gold standard.
Here is why keeping it simple with single-electrode iridium plugs works
best:
Unobstructed Spark: Under turbo boost, the air-fuel mixture is
compressed tightly into the cylinder. A single ground strap gives the
spark a clean, wide-open path to ignite that mixture without blocking
the ﬂame.
Long-Lasting: Iridium has an extremely high melting point, meaning
the electrode won't wear down quickly, even under high heat and
boost pressure. You get a consistent, strong spark for tens of
thousands of kilometers.
Zero Gimmicks: Multi-point or split-tip plugs sound high-tech, but in
reality, they add unnecessary cluter inside the combustion chamber
and can cause misﬁres under boost.
By sticking to a proper single-electrode NGK Iridium plug matched to your
engine, you ensure a rock-solid, reliable spark whether you're idling in
traﬃc or pushing hard through the gears.

Since im opening the engine what other internal upgrades
can i do for cheap
Since you are already opening up the engine block during the build, this is
the golden window to do internal work. Once the engine is closed up and
bolted into the Buick, opening it back up is a massive pain.
However, because your goal is a reliable, long-lasting daily driver that hits
33+ MPG, you want to avoid expensive, high-strung race mods (like wild
camshafs or forged stroker kits) that destroy low-end torque and eat your
budget.
Here are the cheap, high-value internal upgrades you can do while the
engine is open that directly improve durability, reliability, and smooth
power:
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 174 of 425

1. Upgrade the Oil Pump & Relief Valve Shimming
What it is: Inspecting the oil pump, replacing worn gears, and
optionally placing a small shim (washer) in the oil pressure relief valve
spring to slightly increase oil pressure.
Why it maters (Cheap & Eﬀective): Turbo engines generate a lot of
heat and demand serious oil pressure to keep the turbo bearings and
valvetrain alive. A fresh oil pump setup or a cheap shim ensures rock-
solid oil pressure at hot idle and high RPMs, protecting the engine
from premature wear for pennies.
2. Multi-Layer Steel (MLS) Head Gasket & Head Studs
What it is: Upgrading to a quality multi-layer steel head gasket paired
with heavy-duty head studs (like ARP or equivalent local high-tensile
bolts) rather than cheap stock-style head bolts.
Why it maters (Cheap & Eﬀective): When you run a turbo, cylinder
pressures go up. A stock head gasket can blow under boost, leaking
coolant into the cylinders and ruining your engine. An MLS gasket and
strong head studs lock the cylinder head down permanently, giving
you total peace of mind at higher boost levels without breaking the
bank.
3. Clean and Polish the Cylinder Head Ports (Mild De-burrying)
What it is: While the cylinder head is oﬀ, take a small sanding roll or
burr to clean up rough casting ﬂash, sharp edges, and factory
mismatch in the intake and exhaust ports. (You aren't doing a full,
expensive professional port job—just smoothing things out).
Why it maters (Cheap & Eﬀective): It takes a litle bit of sweat equity
(time) rather than money. Smoothing the rough edges helps air ﬂow
more smoothly into and out of the cylinders, slightly improving turbo
spool time and eﬃciency for zero parts cost.
4 New Valve Stem Seals and Valve Lapping
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 175 of 425

4. New Valve Stem Seals and Valve Lapping
What it is: Replacing the rubber valve stem seals and using a mild
grinding compound to "lap" the valves into their seats so they seal
100% perfectly.
Why it maters (Cheap & Eﬀective): Old valve seals dry out and leak,
causing the engine to burn oil (smoke on startup) and foul your spark
plugs. Valve lapping ensures zero compression loss past the valves. It
costs very litle in parts, prevents oil consumption, and restores
factory-fresh compression.
What Internal Upgrades to SKIP (To Save Money & Keep
Reliability)
Since you are on a budget and building a daily driver, skip these unless you
are chasing massive drag-strip power:
Forged Racing Pistons & Rods: Unless you plan to run massive,
engine-shatering boost (over 300+ HP), the strong factory iron-block
internals can easily handle your 210–230 HP daily driver goal. Save
your cash.
Afermarket Performance Camshafs: Aggressive cams shif power
way up high in the RPM band, making the car laggy and miserable to
drive in traﬃc while destroying your fuel economy. Keep the stock
camshaf for smooth low-end torque.
Summary of Your Engine Assembly Checklist
When the engine is apart, spend your money on an MLS head gasket,
heavy-duty head studs, new valve seals, and a refreshed oil pump. Put in
a litle elbow grease cleaning up the ports, and your botom-end and top-
end will be bulletproof, ensuring that "runs forever" reliability you're looking
for.

Yes, it can technically get even beter, but we are now entering the
territory of diminishing returns and added cost/complexity.
If you want to squeeze out that ﬁnal 5% of ultimate performance and
eﬃciency, here are the advanced, professional-tier steps you could take—
along with why they cost more or add extra hassle:
1. Thermal Barrier Coatings & Exhaust Wrap (The Heat Trap)
What it is: Applying ceramic thermal barrier coating to the exhaust
manifold and turbine housing, or wrapping the exhaust pipes.
Why it helps: It keeps the heat inside the exhaust gases. Hoter
exhaust gases expand faster, which means they force the turbo
turbine to spin up even quicker (reducing turbo lag further). It also
keeps under-hood temperatures lower.
The Catch: It adds extra cost, and poorly wrapped exhaust pipes can
trap moisture and rust out old metal if not done with high-end
materials.
2. A Closed-Loop Wideband O2 Sensor Setup with Flex-Fuel or
Knock Control
What it is: Upgrading your standalone ECU setup to include a real-
time wideband oxygen sensor (like a Bosch LSU 4.9) and a dedicated
knock sensor with automatic timing retard.
Why it helps: This gives your ECU a "brain" that constantly self-adjusts.
If you get lower-quality fuel, the knock sensor instantly detects it and
dials back timing to save the engine. The wideband sensor constantly
monitors your air-fuel ratio so your lean-cruise and boost maps are
always dead-on, maximizing both MPG and safety.
The Catch: Adds hardware costs for sensors and wiring, though it is
strongly recommended for any serious standalone ECU build anyway.

3. Electric Cooling Fan Conversion (Thermostatically
Controlled)
What it is: Ditching the heavy mechanical engine-driven fan (if the
donor setup has one) and replacing it with a modern, slim electric fan
controlled by your standalone ECU.
Why it helps: A mechanical fan constantly siphons a few horsepower
straight oﬀ the crankshaf just to spin. An electric fan only turns on
when the engine actually needs cooling, freeing up parasitic drag
(more wheel horsepower) and helping the engine warm up faster to
optimal operating temperatures (beter fuel eﬃciency).
The Catch: Requires custom wiring and a reliable relay setup.

To jump from 230 HP to 250 HP safely, you can't just cross your ﬁngers and
turn up a manual knob. You need:

running out of breath will just blow hot air at 250 HP, killing your power
gains and risking engine knock. You need a slightly larger, modern ball-

overheating the intake air.
Upgraded Intercooler Core: Because you are pushing more boost, a

to keep that compressed air ice-cold.
Slightly Larger Injectors: Bump your fuel injector size up slightly from
550cc to around 650cc–700cc to make sure they have plenty of
headroom at high RPMs without running at 100% duty cycle.

Yes, absolutely. Because of how turbocharged engines work, fuel
economy is controlled by how much air the engine breathes when you are
light on the throtle (in a vacuum).
As long as you are cruising on the highway out of boost (at 110 km/h in
5th gear), the bigger turbo isn't working hard, and your standalone
ECU's lean-cruise map will still sip fuel just as eﬃciently.
You will only burn more fuel when you smash the throtle and force the
turbo into high boost. So your highway economy stays near 30–33
MPG but your right foot becomes a much more dramatic switch

To understand how power scales with fuel economy, we have to look at

Because of this, your "maximum power" and your "lowest/highest MPG"
don't ﬁght each other the way they do in old carbureted V8 engines. Here is

1. The Highest MPG State (The Highway Cruise)
Power Output Being Used: 15 to 25 HP (Regardless of whether your
engine is built to max out at 200 HP or 250 HP).
What the engine is doing: You are cruising at 110 km/h in 5th gear. The
engine is siting in a deep manifold vacuum. The turbo isn't spinning
hard, and your standalone ECU's lean-cruise map is commanding a
lean air-fuel ratio (up to 16:1 or 17:1) with heavy ignition timing advance.
The Result: 33 to 35+ MPG (~6.7 to 7.0 L/100km).
The takeaway: Because you only need ~20 horsepower to keep a 1,200
kg car rolling smoothly down the highway, your peak engine
horsepower ceiling has zero negative impact on your highway MPG.
Whether you build for 230 HP or 250 HP, your highway fuel economy
stays identical if you stay out of the boost.
2. The Lowest MPG State (Maximum Power / Full Boost)
Power Output Being Used: 250 HP (Maximum output at 14–16 PSI of
boost).
What the engine is doing: You smash the throtle to the ﬂoor. The
turbo aggressively spools up, forcing massive volumes of compressed
air into the cylinders. To prevent the engine from melting under
extreme heat and pressure, the standalone ECU dumps fuel, enriching
the mixture (around 11.5:1 to 12:1) and pulling back ignition timing.
The Result: 8 to 12 MPG (or even lower if you are WOT on a twisty

The Result: 8 to 12 MPG (or even lower if you are WOT on a twisty

Can i go higher hp?
You can go higher, but you hit a hard engineering wall where the rules
change completely.
If you want to push past 250 HP while keeping your stock engine internals
untouched, you are stepping into a very dangerous gray area. Here is what
happens if you try to squeeze more power out of a stock-botom-end 4-
cylinder engine:
The Hard Limits of Stock Internals
The Stock Connecting Rods: Factory rods are built to handle normal
factory loads. Around 260 to 270 HP (depending on how aggressive
the torque curve hits), peak cylinder pressures under boost become
high enough to bend or snap a stock rod. When a rod snaps, it
punches a hole straight through the engine block, destroying the
entire motor instantly.
The "Grenade" Factor: To run higher power on stock internals, tuners
have to retard ignition timing and run rich mixtures to prevent engine
knock (detonation). But one bad tank of local fuel, or one split-second
of a failing sensor, and the engine detonates and destroys itself.

Why We Stopped at 250 HP
The reason 250 HP is the gold standard for this speciﬁc build strategy is
simple: it is 100% safe on factory internals.
You get all the performance you could possibly want for a daily driver
(a sub-6.5-second 0–100 km/h sprint).
Your stock block, pistons, and rods live a long, happy life.
You keep your build budget low because you don't have to buy
expensive forged race parts or pay for heavy engine block machining.
The Only Way to Go Higher Safely
If you truly want more power (like 280, 300, or 320+ HP), you cannot do it
on a whim. You have to cross the threshold into a fully built motor:
1. Tear down the engine block.
2. Buy and install forged pistons and H-beam connecting rods ($1,000+).
3. Pay a machine shop to bore, hone, and line-hone the block.
4. Upgrade the fuel system (bigger pump, lines, and 750cc+ injectors).
5. Accept lower highway fuel economy because a larger turbo and
richer fueling will pull your highway MPG down closer to the mid-20s.

How to tune for that
Achieving 250 HP, 350 Nm of torque, a 6.5-second 0–100 km/h time, and
35+ MPG highway on stock internal components requires precise
calibration of your standalone ECU.
Because you have two completely diﬀerent operating states (Highway
Cruising vs. Full Boost), your tuner will need to build a dual-map system
with speciﬁc parameters. Here is the exact tuning guide to make those
numbers a reality:
Phase 1: Tuning the ECU for 35+ MPG (The Highway Cruise
Map)
When you are cruising at 110 km/h in 5th gear, the engine is under high
vacuum (out of boost). This is where you extract your 35 MPG.
Air-Fuel Ratio (AFR): Command a lean cruise AFR of 16.0:1 to 17.0:1
under low-load manifold vacuum. Traditional stoichiometric is 14.7:1,
but modern widebands (Bosch LSU 4.9) allow safely leaning it out
during highway cruising to drastically cut fuel consumption.
Ignition Timing Advance: Add signiﬁcant ignition timing advance
(around 38° to 45° BTDC) in the high-vacuum cruise cells. Because the
air-fuel mixture is lean, it burns slower and needs to be lit earlier to
extract maximum energy before the exhaust valve opens.
Closed-Loop Target: Lock your closed-loop O2 correction to maintain
stability strictly during light-throtle cruising, ensuring it instantly
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 203 of 425

switches back to safety mode the moment your foot touches the
accelerator.
Phase 2: Tuning the ECU for 250 HP & 350 Nm (The High-Boost
Power Map)
The second you press the accelerator down, the ECU switches gears to
deliver that 350 Nm wall of torque and 250 HP.
Boost Control & Target: Set your electronic boost controller
(managed by the standalone ECU) to peak at 14 to 16 PSI. Bring the
boost in early (around 3,000 RPM) to instantly generate that 350 Nm
of torque down low, making the lightweight car feel explosive.
Power AFR (Rich Mixture): Under full boost, drop your AFR down to a
safe, rich 11.5:1 to 11.8:1. This extra fuel acts as a liquid coolant inside
the combustion chambers, protecting your stock pistons and exhaust
valves from melting under high heat.
Ignition Timing Retard Under Boost: Pull back your ignition timing
aggressively as boost builds (dropping down toward 12° to 16° BTDC at
peak boost) to completely eliminate destructive engine knock
(detonation).
Phase 3: The Automated Safety Nets (Mandatory for Stock
Internals)
To safely push 350 Nm through stock factory connecting rods without
blowing up the engine, your tune must include active digital safeguards:
Active Knock Control: Set up your standalone ECU with a real-time
knock sensor. Program the sofware to instantly pull 2° to 4° of
ignition timing the millisecond a hint of detonation is detected, saving
your stock botom end from a bent rod.
Over-Boost & Exhaust Gas Temperature (EGT) Failsafes: Program a
hard boost-cut if boost spikes past 17 PSI, and monitor your wideband
O2 sensor so that if the AFR ever leans out under boost, the ECU
instantly cuts throtle or dumps extra fuel.
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 204 of 425

Summary of the Tuning Blueprint
1. Low Load (Vacuum): Lean AFR (16.5:1) + High Timing (40°+) = 35+
MPG
2. High Load (Boost / 15 PSI): Rich AFR (11.6:1) + Controlled Timing (14°) +
Knock Retard = 250 HP / 350 Nm / 6.5s 0-100 km/h
Hand this exact strategy to your dyno tuner, and they will be able to lock in
this "best-of-both-worlds" performance proﬁle precisely. Ready to map out
the next step of the build?

Can i rally with that or go ligh gravel off roadi g
Taking this car onto light gravel roads, graded dirt trails, or light oﬀ-roading
is totally doable and a lot of fun, but you need to make one major
adjustment.
Trying to run a traditional, low-slung street restomod suspension on rough
gravel is a recipe for cracked oil pans, bent exhaust pipes, and destroyed
rocker panels. However, if you build it as a "Safari Restomod" (keeping all
your performance, torque, and eﬃciency specs exactly the same), you can
transform it into an incredible all-terrain cruiser.
Here is what it takes to make your Buick Skylark survive light gravel and rally
roads without losing its daily-driver manners:
1. The Suspension Adjustment: Lif It Slightly & Protect the
Botom
Ride Height: Instead of lowering the car or keeping it at factory
height, add 1 to 2 inches of lif using slightly stiﬀer, taller springs or
custom spacers. This gives you the ground clearance needed to clear
rocks and ruts without botoming out.
Skid Plate (Mandatory): Fabricate a thick aluminum or steel skid plate
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 209 of 425

to bolt directly underneath the front engine crossmember and protect
your oil pan and intercooler piping from ﬂying rocks.
Upgraded Dampers: Use heavy-duty, slightly beeﬁer shock absorbers
(like Bilstein or equivalent gas shocks) that can soak up repetitive
washboard bumps without fading or bouncing out of control.
2. Wheel and Tire Setup (The Dirt GrIP)
Smaller Wheels, Taller Sidewalls: Instead of large rims with rubber
band tires, drop down to a 15-inch or 16-inch wheel. This lets you run a
thick, meaty tire sidewall (all-terrain or gravel rally tires). The tall
sidewall acts as a secondary cushion, absorbing impacts so they don't
crack your alloy wheels or ratle the classic chassis apart.
Tire Choice: Look for a mild all-terrain tire or a gravel rally compound
that still has decent road manners for your 35 MPG highway
commutes.
3. How the 250 HP / 350 Nm Setup Handles Gravel
The Torque Advantage: That 350 Nm of low-end torque coming in
early (around 3,000 RPM) is actually an absolute blast on loose dirt. It
allows you to steer the car with the throtle, kicking the rear end out
smoothly on dirt corners like a true rally car.
Open vs. Limited-Slip Diﬀerential (LSD): If you plan to play on gravel
ofen, make sure your rear axle has a Limited-Slip Diﬀerential (LSD) or
a locker. An open diﬀerential will just spin one inside rear tire uselessly
when you try to accelerate on loose dirt or mud.
The Verdict
Can you do light gravel and oﬀ-roading? Yes, absolutely. A lifed, skid-
plated, high-torque "Safari Skylark" running on meatier tires with a 250 HP
punch is one of the coolest builds you can possibly make.
It handles potholes, bad roads, and dirt tracks eﬀortlessly while still giving
you your 35 MPG highway cruise on the way home. Do you want to bake a

cabin comfort, your exhaust piping needs to be tuned for ﬂow without
being loud and obnoxious.
The Setup: Have your local exhaust shop fabricate a 2.5-inch to 3-inch
downpipe oﬀ the turbo (straight-piped or with a high-ﬂow cat),
running into a large, high-quality local or imported muﬄer (like a
baﬄed twin-chamber muﬄer).
Why it works: A 2.5–3 inch pipe lets the turbo spool up quickly and
breathe easily at high RPMs, while a proper baﬄed muﬄer ensures
your highway cruise at 35 MPG remains whisper-quiet inside the cabin
—no annoying drone.
3. Header Wrap or Ceramic Coating
The Budget Move: Wrap your custom exhaust manifold and downpipe
in thermal exhaust wrap or use a local ceramic coating service if
available.
Why it works: Under-hood temperatures in classic engine bays get
extremely hot, especially in warm climates. Keeping the heat inside the

With modern electornics like head lights and tail lights and

Upgrading to modern electronics—like LED headlights, LED tail lights,
modern switchgear, and digital or upgraded gauges—is the ﬁnal touch
that brings a classic car into the 21st century without sacriﬁcing its vintage
charm.
Because classic car wiring can be britle and prone to electrical gremlins,
integrating modern lighting and electronics needs to be done smartly. Here
is how you can do it cheaply, reliably, and using parts or techniques
available locally:
1. LED Headlights & Conversion Bulbs
What to do: Swap your dim old sealed-beam headlights for modern
H4 conversion housings paired with bright H4 LED bulbs (or drop-in
LED headlight units that ﬁt standard classic bucket sizes).
Why it helps: They draw a fraction of the electrical current compared
to old halogen or incandescent bulbs (saving your alternator and
wiring harness), while turning night driving on dark highways or unlit
country trails into daylight.
Cost & Sourcing: Extremely cheap and widely available at local auto
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 223 of 425

Cost & Sourcing: Extremely cheap and widely available at local auto
parts shops.
2. Modern LED Tail Lights & Signal Bulbs
What to do: Upgrade your rear brake, running, and turn signal bulbs to
crisp, bright LED equivalents. You can also look into custom LED panel
inserts or keep your factory red lenses and just swap in high-output
LED bulbs.
Why it helps: LEDs light up instantly (giving drivers behind you an
extra fraction of a second to react), pull almost zero power, and never
burn out.
The Look: Keeps the classic exterior look completely authentic when
the lights are oﬀ, but gives a sharp, modern glow when active.
3. The Hidden Electrical Upgrade: A Modern Fuse Box & Relays
What to do: Classic cars ofen route all their electrical power directly
through the headlight switch or ignition switch, which can melt old
wires. Have an auto electrician install a modern blade-fuse box and a
few cheap automotive relays for your headlights and electric cooling
fan.
Why it helps: This is the secret to bulletproof reliability. It protects
your new electronics from short circuits, prevents electrical ﬁres, and
ensures your modern headlights and electric fan get full, uninhibited
voltage directly from the batery.
4. Modern Conveniences (Kept Stealthy)
USB Charging & Bluetooth: Keep your dashboard looking clean and
stock, but tuck a hidden dual-USB charging port under the dash or
inside the glove box, and install a hidden Bluetooth ampliﬁer so you
can stream music from your phone straight to your speakers without
ruining the classic radio look.

How to maximize my mpg like modern cars and be silent and
soft on drive too
Achieving modern fuel economy, whisper-quiet cabin reﬁnement, and a
plush, compliant ride in a high-horsepower, lifed restomod comes down to
a few speciﬁc engineering choices during the build.
While classic cars naturally suﬀer from poor aerodynamics, loud sheet
metal vibration, and high-revving gearing, you can engineer modern luxury
and eﬃciency directly into the platorm:
1. Maximizing MPG (Beating the Commuter Cars on Eﬃciency)
High horsepower doesn't have to mean terrible gas mileage if you manage
how the engine delivers power:
Electronic Fuel Injection (EFI) & Precise Tuning: Instead of a raw, fuel-
dumping setup, run a programmable engine management system with
a dual-map tune. Map 1 gives you full-boost 250 HP aggression; Map 2
leans out the fuel mixture signiﬁcantly during highway cruising to
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 252 of 425

maximize eﬃciency.
Modern Overdrive or Tall Final-Drive Gearing: Classic cars ofen have
a 1:1 ﬁnal drive ratio that screams at high RPMs on the highway,
burning massive amounts of fuel. Adding an overdrive gear or tall
diﬀerential ratio drops your highway cruising RPM way down, allowing
the engine to sip fuel at 100 km/h just like a modern commuter.
Aerodynamic Smoothness: Keeping your 3-4mm steel skid plate ﬂat
and ﬂush underneath prevents turbulent air from geting trapped
under the chassis, reducing highway drag and improving fuel
economy.
2. Achieving a Silent Cabin (Killing Noise, Vibration, &
Harshness - NVH)
Old cars are loud because bare metal acts like a giant speaker cone for
road and exhaust noise. You ﬁx this with a modern insulation layer:
Butyl Sound Deadening Mats: Line the entire bare interior ﬂoor,
doors, roof, and trunk with adhesive butyl mats. This eliminates sheet-
metal resonance and tin-can ratles entirely.
Closed-Cell Foam & Thermal Barriers: Layer closed-cell foam over the
butyl mat on the ﬁrewall and ﬂoorboards. This blocks engine heat and
completely muﬄes high-frequency tire and wind noise.
Exhaust Tuning (No Drone): When your local fabricator builds the 2.5–
3" exhaust, mandate a large, high-volume turbo resonator/muﬄer. It
keeps the exhaust completely quiet and stealthy when you're just
cruising around town, but opens up with a deep note under heavy
boost.
3. Delivering a Sof, Compliant Ride (Despite the Lif)
A lifed safari build can easily ride like a stiﬀ tractor if you aren't careful, but
you can maintain a Cadillac-like plushness:
Progressive-Rate Heavy-Duty Springs: Instead of stiﬀ, cheap
industrial springs, source progressive-rate domestic springs. They
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 253 of 425

industrial springs, source progressive rate domestic springs. They
oﬀer a sof, comfortable absorption rate over small bumps and
potholes, but ﬁrm up progressively when you hit a deep rut or take a
sharp corner.
Gas-Charged Modern Dampers (Shocks): Pair the springs with
matched gas-charged shock absorbers. They control body roll and
eliminate bouncy, uncontrolled oscillations, giving you a smooth,
planted highway glide.
High-Proﬁle Radial Tires: Avoid rubber-band low-proﬁle tires. Sticking
to 15-inch wheels wrapped in meaty, high-sidewall all-terrain tires
provides natural air cushioning that swallows up rough pavement
impacts before they ever reach the chassis.
By combining a lean highway engine map, tall cruising gears, full interior
sound-deadening mats, and progressive suspension dampening, you get a
machine that cruises silently, sips fuel on long trips, ﬂoats comfortably over
rough roads, but transforms into an aggressive, torque-heavy drif monster
the second you step on the throtle.

1. The "Cheap & Easy" Way to Beter MPG

standalone race ECU, use a factory-pulled multi-port fuel injection

What about 35+ mpg?
Hiting 35+ MPG (around 6.7 L/100km) in a custom, heavy-chassis, 250-
horsepower turbocharged restomod is a massive ask, but it is
mathematically and mechanically possible if you engineer the car
speciﬁcally for hyper-eﬃciency during highway cruising.
A traditional heavy cruiser like a Skylark naturally ﬁghts air resistance and
weight, but you can achieve modern eco-car highway mileage by applying
strict eﬃciency strategies:
1. The Overdrive & Tall Final Drive Strategy (Crucial)
To hit 35 MPG, your engine cannot be spinning at 2,500+ RPM on the
highway.
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 262 of 425

g
y
The Fix: You need a modern multi-speed automatic or manual
overdrive transmission (like a common domestic or adapted 4-to-5
speed overdrive setup) paired with a tall rear diﬀerential gear ratio
(e.g., 3.08:1 or lower).
The Result: At 100 km/h on the highway, your engine will drop down to
a lazy 1,500 to 1,700 RPM, sipping fuel instead of burning it.
2. The "Dual-Map" Lean-Burn EFI & Boost Management
A turbocharger is your best friend for eﬃciency if you stay out of the
boost.
Staying in Vacuum: Under light highway cruising, the turbo acts as a
mild restriction unless managed. With a programmable EFI system,
your tuner sets up a lean-burn highway cruise map that aggressively
leans out the air-fuel ratio (up to 16:1 or 17:1 AFR) when manifold
pressure is in deep vacuum.
The Foot Discipline: The moment you dip your foot into the throtle,
the turbo spools, the AFR richens, and your power comes alive. But for
35 MPG, you treat the gas pedal like an eggshell on the highway.
3. Aerodynamic & Rolling Resistance Reduction
At highway speeds, 70% of your engine's power is used just to push air out
of the way and overcome tire friction.
The Flush Skid Plate: Your 3–4mm steel skid plate needs to mount
completely ﬂat and ﬂush against the front crossmember and frame
rails, creating a smooth underbody belly pan that prevents air from
geting trapped inside the engine bay and creating drag.
Low Rolling Resistance Highway Tires: While you want meaty all-
terrain sidewalls for your safari lif, aggressive knobby treads destroy
fuel economy due to high rolling resistance. To actually hit 35 MPG on
long trips, you'd want a set of highway-terrain (H/T) radial tires or strict
highway pressures, accepting a minor trade-oﬀ in deep mud traction.
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 263 of 425

The Reality Check
Can a heavy, lifed, turbocharged 250 HP restomod hit 35+ MPG?
On the highway, cruising empty at a steady 90–100 km/h with tall
gearing and a lean tune? Yes, absolutely. Modern fuel injection and
low-RPM overdrive make it achievable.
In city stop-and-go traﬃc or while heavy on the turbo boost? No.
Expect city numbers to drop down to 14–18 MPG as the turbo spools
and fuel injectors work overtime.
If you want that 35+ MPG highway capability built right into your 5 billion
Toman budget, it comes down to prioritizing an overdrive transmission
swap and a precision lean-burn highway tune during Phase 3 and Phase 4.
Are you ready to map out how to source that overdrive transmission setup
locally?
Yes i want it to check all check boxes with minimum weight
and max mpg
To achieve minimum weight and maximum MPG (targeting that 35+
highway MPG mark) while keeping every single performance, comfort,
and oﬀ-road checkbox ticked, we have to engineer a strict "Weight-Diet &
Aero-Eﬃciency" strategy right into the build sheet.
Here is how you hit that sweet spot without blowing your 5 billion Toman
budget:
1. The Weight-Diet Strategy (Targeting ~1,480 kg Total Weight)
A lighter car accelerates faster, handles sharper, and sips signiﬁcantly less
fuel Instead of adding heavy dead weight we aggressively trim fat where it

Interior Strip & Replace: Completely strip out the old, heavy,

2. The 35+ MPG Highway Eﬃciency Blueprint
To make a 250 HP turbocharged engine cruise at 35+ MPG (around 6.5–6.7
L/100km) on the highway:
The Overdrive Transmission Swap: Lock in a modern 4-speed
automatic with lock-up torque converter or a 5-speed manual
overdrive transmission from a common domestic donor. Paired with a
tall highway diﬀerential ratio (e.g., 3.08:1), your highway cruising RPM

or factory-adapted EFI system will feature a switchable map. When
cruising on the highway, it engages a lean-burn air-fuel ratio (up to
16.5:1 AFR) and pulls ignition advance timing slightly to maximize
thermal eﬃciency while keeping manifold pressure in deep vacuum.
Highway-Terrain (H/T) Tread Discipline: To avoid the massive rolling-
resistance drag of heavy knobby mud tires on long highway runs, wrap
your wheels in a hybrid All-Terrain / Highway-Terrain (A/T-H/T hybrid)
tire. It gives you enough shoulder block for dirt and snow sliding, but a
smooth center rib for low rolling resistance on the asphalt.

At an optimized total weight of 1,500 kg, the weight distribution for your

The Breakdown: Optimized Weight Distribution
Front Axle: ~795 kg (53%)
Rear Axle: ~705 kg (47%)
Why This Distribution is Perfect for Your Goals:
1. The Drif Advantage (53/47 Balance):
Traditional classic American cruisers are notoriously nose-heavy
(ofen pushing 56% to 58% up front), which causes aggressive
understeer.
By leaning out the front end, using a lighter custom turbo
manifold setup, and shifing heavy components backward (like
relocating the batery to the trunk and housing the robust
mechanical LSD axle out back), you pull the center of gravity
closer to the middle. This allows the rear tires to break traction
progressively and predictably when you want to drif, rather than
ﬁghting sudden snap oversteer or stubborn understeer.
2. Safari & All-Weather Traction:
Having 47% of the vehicle's weight siting directly over the driven
rear wheels is a massive win for oﬀ-road and winter traction.
Combined with your mechanical LSD, those rear tires will bite
hard into dirt, mud, and snow instead of spinning freely like a
lightweight, tail-light sports car.
3. High-Speed Straight-Line Stability:
The 53% front weight bias ensures that at 100+ km/h highway
speeds, your steering remains heavy, planted, and calm—
preventing the front end from feeling "ﬂoaty" or light when you're
cruising on long stretches.
This balance turns a heavy classic body into a nimble neutral-handling

This balance turns a heavy classic body into a nimble, neutral-handling

So will 50/50 ruin the performance?
No, a 50/50 weight distribution will not ruin your performance—in fact,
it is the holy grail for handling, cornering, and balanced driving
dynamics.
Virtually every high-performance sports car, track weapon, and
professional drif car (like BMW M-cars or Porsche 911s in their respective
setups) strives for that exact 50/50 balance.
However, because your build is a "Safari-Drif" machine that needs to
handle loose dirt, snow, and gravel in addition to pavement, shifing the
weight too neutral or tail-heavy changes how the car behaves. Here is how
a true 50/50 split aﬀects your speciﬁc setup:
1. The Good: Why 50/50 Feels Incredible
Neutral Cornering: With equal weight over both axles, the car won't
stubbornly push its nose wide (understeer) or violently snap its rear
end out unexpectedly (snap oversteer). It will rotate predictably
through corners.
Balanced Braking: When you slam on the brakes, all four tires share
the deceleration workload more evenly, stopping the car shorter and
ﬂater without diving aggressively on the front suspension.
Predictable Drifing: When you initiate a drif, a 50/50 chassis allows
you to slide smoothly with all four wheels communicating what they're
doing through the steering wheel. It's much easier to catch and
control mid-drif.
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 268 of 425

2. The Trade-Oﬀ: Oﬀ-Road & Deep Snow Traction
While 50/50 is a dream for asphalt and dry drif tracks, there is one minor
compromise when you take a Safari build oﬀ-road:
Rear Bite in Loose Terrain: When driving through deep mud, thick
sand, or heavy winter snow, having slightly more weight over the rear
wheels (like a 47F / 53R setup) acts like a shovel, pushing the driven
tires down into the loose surface for maximum forward bite.
At a pure 50/50 split, you still have massive traction thanks to your
mechanical LSD and meaty tires, but you rely slightly more on tire
tread depth rather than pure static weight over the rear axle when
crawling through sloppy terrain.
The Verdict
Achieving a 50/50 weight distribution will not ruin your performance at all
—it will make the car handle like a world-class machine on pavement
and twisty roads.
If you want to nudge it toward a pure 50/50 balance (750 kg front / 750 kg
rear), you can easily do it by relocating the batery and windshield washer
ﬂuid reservoir to the trunk during assembly.
Would you like to lock in the 50/50 weight target for the ﬁnal blueprint, or
keep that slight rearward bias for extra snow/mud traction?
So how to make it 50/50
Achieving a true 50/50 weight distribution on a classic chassis like a
Skylark requires moving heavy components from the front and middle
toward the rear to balance out the heavy cast-iron engine block siting up
front.
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 269 of 425

You can accomplish this using a cheap, garage-friendly weight-shifing
strategy without needing fancy exotic parts:
1. Relocate the Batery to the Trunk
How to do it: Move the heavy lead-acid or AGM starter batery from
the engine bay and mount it securely in the rear trunk (ideally over or
slightly behind the rear axle on the passenger side to oﬀset driver
weight).
Weight Shif: Instantly transfers 15 to 20 kg oﬀ the front nose and
plants it right over the rear wheels.
2. Relocate Fluid Reservoirs
Washer Fluid & Coolant Overﬂow: Move the windshield washer ﬂuid
tank and coolant overﬂow tanks out of the front corners of the engine
bay and mount them in the trunk or inner fender wells behind the front
axle line.
Weight Shif: Trims a few crucial kilograms from the extreme front
overhang, improving turn-in response.
3. Optimize the Fuel Cell / Gas Tank Placement
How to do it: If you are modifying or replacing the fuel tank during the
build, position a fuel cell as far forward into the trunk space as safety
permits—right up against the rear seat bulkhead.
Weight Shif: Gasoline weighs roughly 0.75 kg per liter. A full 60-liter
tank siting right over or just behind the rear axle keeps the balance
consistent whether the tank is full or nearly empty.
4. Smart Placement of Build Hardware
The Sound Deadening & Audio: Heavy materials like your DIY sound-
deadening bitumen mats and any audio ampliﬁers or subwoofers
naturally add weight toward the cabin ﬂoor and trunk which helps pull
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 270 of 425

naturally add weight toward the cabin ﬂoor and trunk, which helps pull
the center of gravity rearward away from the front nose.
The Skid Plate Balance: Your 3–4mm steel front skid plate adds
necessary front-end protection, but using aluminum brackets for the
rear mounting points helps trim a tiny bit of forward weight.
By combining a trunk-mounted batery, relocated ﬂuid tanks, and smart
fuel/weight placement, you can easily drag that front-heavy nose bias
back until the scales read a dead-even 750 kg Front / 750 kg Rear (50/50).
With the weight distribution, MPG targets, 250 HP turbo output, and
budget all mathematically balanced, where should we start laying down the
actual build steps for the garage?
How would car run on these scales
With a dead-even 50/50 weight distribution (750 kg Front / 750 kg Rear)
at a lightweight 1,500 kg total, your power-to-weight ratio actually
improves to 166.7 HP per tonne.
Here is exactly how the car will behave on the road, in the dirt, and when
you push it hard:
1. On Asphalt (Cornering & Grip)
Razor-Sharp Turn-In: Because you’ve pulled dead weight oﬀ the front
nose, the car won't plow or push wide when you snap the steering
wheel into a sharp corner. The front tires bite instantly, making it feel
remarkably agile for a classic chassis.
Neutral Balance: Mid-corner behavior is completely balanced. You
can feel all four tires working equally to hold the line, giving you
massive conﬁdence through twisty backroads.
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM

2. Under Acceleration & Launch (With the LSD)
Squat and Bite: The moment you hammer the throtle, rearward
weight transfer naturally shifs even more load onto those rear tires.
Combined with your mechanical LSD, the rear squat ensures both
tires hook up hard instead of one wheel spinning uselessly.
Zero Wheel-Hop Drama: The balanced load prevents the rear axle
from skipping or hopping over uneven pavement under heavy boost.
3. When Drifing & Sliding
Predictable Rotation: A 50/50 car is a drif instructor's dream. When
you break traction, the rear end steps out smoothly and progressively
rather than snapping violently.
Easy to Catch: Because the polar moment of inertia is centered,
catching the slide with a quick counter-steer and modulating the
throtle feels eﬀortless. You can hold long, controlled power-slides
with total predictability.
4. Oﬀ-Road and Safari Compliance
Balanced Impact Absorption: When you hit a rough dirt rut, a bump,
or a dip on a safari trail, all four corners of the progressive suspension
compress evenly. The car stays ﬂat and level rather than nose-diving
into the dirt.
Summary
On these scales, your build transforms from a heavy, lazy cruiser into a
perfectly balanced, responsive machine that handles like a modern sports
sedan while keeping its rugged, torque-heavy safari-drif personality.
