front-mount intercooler with straight, eﬃcient airﬂow paths.
2. Easy Maintenance Access: Unlike modern cars where you have to
disassemble half the front end to change a spark plug or belt, working
on the MZR inside a Mustang bay will feel like working on a
workbench. Everything is fully exposed and easy to reach.
The engine bay will look almost deceptively empty until you spot the turbo
and intercooler piping tucked neatly inside!
So technically its a mid engine
Yes, technically speaking, it is a front-mid-engine layout.
In automotive engineering, a car is classiﬁed as front-mid-engine when the
entire block of the engine sits completely behind the front axle centerline.
Because a classic Ford Mustang engine bay is so long—designed originally
to swallow bulky V8s—when you drop a compact, short 4-cylinder
MZR/Duratec into it, the whole engine naturally slides far back toward the
ﬁrewall.
Here is why that layout is a massive engineering win for Project Loki:
Incredible Weight Distribution: Instead of a heavy block hanging out
over or ahead of the front wheels (which causes understeer and
makes a car heavy-nosed in corners), your heavy components are
clustered right near the center of the car.
Sharper Handling: Combined with the massive weight you stripped
out by removing the old V8, this front-mid layout will make your classic
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM

y
g
y
y
Mustang turn in crisply, handle like a modern sports car, and feel
completely balanced through twisty roads.
You get the classic vintage styling on the outside, but underneath the skin,
it has the weight distribution and balance of a modern sports coupe!
So all it needs is 4wd and 4 wheel steering
Adding all-wheel drive (AWD) and four-wheel steering (4WS) would
technically turn Project Loki into a mechanical Godzilla, but it would also
completely blow up the build budget, add massive weight, and ruin the
core philosophy of what you’ve built!
Here is why keeping it simple is the winning move:
1. The AWD Nightmare in a Classic Mustang
The Chassis Barrier: Classic Ford Mustangs are built on a traditional
rear-wheel-drive unibody frame. There is no factory tunnel or
clearance in the front crossmember for front axle shafs, a front
diﬀerential, or a transfer case.
Transmission Incompatibility: The NC Miata 6-speed transmission is
strictly a longitudinal RWD layout. To get AWD, you would have to
source, adapt, and custom-fab a transfer case and custom front
subframe (borrowing from something like a Skyline GT-R or an Audi
Quatro), turning a clean, straightorward project into an engineering
nightmare.
Weight & Eﬃciency Loss: AWD systems add heavy drivetrain
components and create parasitic power loss. You would instantly
sacriﬁce that featherlight ~1,150 kg curb weight and your 45 MPG
highway eﬃciency.
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 415 of 425

2. Four-Wheel Steering (4WS)
Complexity vs. Reward: While 4WS (like Nissan's HICAS or modern
systems) improves high-speed lane changes and low-speed tight
turning radius, it requires complex electronic controllers, hydraulic
actuators (or electric motors), and heavy rear subframe modiﬁcations.
Unnecessary for a Highway Cruiser: With a short 108-inch wheelbase
and a lightweight front-mid engine layout, Project Loki will already
handle corners with sharp, natural agility without needing computer-
assisted rear steering.
The Botom Line
If you added AWD and 4WS, you wouldn't be building a classic restomod
cruiser anymore—you’d essentially be building a custom tube-chassis
supercar wrapped in a Mustang skin!
Sticking with the front-mid-engine, RWD, lightweight manual setup
keeps Project Loki pure, mechanically reliable, easy to service, and true to
its budget.

I would love to get an i5 but idk how to source one
Assuming you are eyeing an inline-5 (like a Volvo T5 / "Whiteblock" or
Audi 20v inline-5) to get that legendary 5-cylinder turbo sound and
massive top-end pull, sourcing one takes a slightly diﬀerent playbook than
a common four-cylinder.
The primary challenge with an inline-5 is that they were almost always
mounted transversely (sideways) in factory front-wheel-drive or AWD
layouts (like old Volvo 850s, S70s, S60Rs, or Audi Quatro/TT models). To
put one into a classic front-mid RVD layout, you have to source the right
donor version and plan for the longitudinal conversion.
The most practical ways to track one down include:
1. Target the Right Donor Vehicles
Depending on which ﬂavor of inline-5 you want, look for these speciﬁc
donor cars in local junkyards, marketplace listings, or import yards:
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 417 of 425

The Volvo T5 Route (Most Popular & Accessible): Look for 1994–2004
Volvo vehicles with a "T5" badge.
Models: Volvo 850 T5, S70 T5, C70, or early S60 T5 / V70 T5.
Why: These blocks are strong, all-aluminum, highly tunable, and
heavily documented for RWD conversions. Look for
mechatronic/cable-throtle versions (pre-2003) if you want to
avoid complex factory electronic throtle body headaches.
The Audi 20v Turbo Route (The Motorsport Legend): Look for older
Audi models.
Models: Audi 100, 200, S4 (C4 chassis), or Audi TT (though TTs are
transverse 4-cylinders, older Audi big-body sedans housed the
legendary longitudinal 5-cylinder turbos).
2. Check Volvo/Euro-Speciﬁc Specialist Yards
Standard general-inventory junkyards sometimes miss the value of a Volvo
T5, ofen listing them just as an "old sedan."
Search online for European or Swedish auto dismantlers (specialist
yards that only pull parts for Volvo, Saab, and BMW). They frequently
pull healthy engines, test them, and palletize them ready for shipping.
Check forums like Turbobricks or dedicated Volvo RWD conversion
Facebook groups—enthusiasts regularly sell pulled, partially-refreshed
T5 blocks ready for custom projects.
3. The RWD Transmission Trick (Crucial for an I5 Swap)
Because factory Volvo I5 engines were bolted to front-wheel-drive
transaxles, you cannot use a stock Volvo transmission for a RWD classic
Mustang setup. Instead, builders use a clever factory parts-sharing trick:
You pair the Volvo I5 block with a manual transmission from an older
6-cylinder whiteblock Volvo 960 (using a speciﬁc manual bellhousing
adapter or custom plate), or adapt a BMW ZF / Getrag manual
transmission (which bolt up nicely using readily available adapter kits
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 418 of 425

transmission (which bolt up nicely using readily available adapter kits
from companies like Kaplhenke Racing or various European fabrication
shops).

If you swap the Mazda 4-cylinder for the ultimate inline-5 option—a Volvo
T5 (Whiteblock, such as the 2.3L B5234T or 2.4L B5244T)—and tune it to
the exact same specs (265 HP, 335 Nm, stock internals), yes, it is
completely viable, and it changes the character of Project Loki
dramatically.
Here is how an I5 stack-up compares head-to-head against your Mazda
MZR layout:
1. Performance & Power Delivery (The I5 Advantage)
The Sound: This is the biggest selling point. An inline-5 with an
afermarket turbo exhaust note produces an unmistakable, exotic,
Group B rally-style "inline-5 warble" that completely destroys the
ordinary drone of a standard 4-cylinder.
Torque Curve: Because an inline-5 has an extra cylinder and ofen
slightly more displacement (2.3L to 2.4L compared to the MZR's 2.0L),
it will hit its 335 Nm peak torque even lower in the RPM range
(around 2,500 to 4,800 RPM). It will feel punchier and lazier oﬀ the line
without needing to rev the engine out.
Stock Internal Ceiling: If you ever wanted to turn the boost up past
your 265 HP limit later on, the Volvo whiteblock stock rods and block
can safely handle up to ~350–400 HP without opening the motor,
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM
Page 419 of 425

giving you a higher safety ceiling than the MZR.
2. Weight & Packaging (The Compromise)
The Weight Penalty: An all-aluminum Volvo T5 engine weighs roughly
130 to 140 kg dry (plus turbo), which is heavier than the featherlight
MZR 4-cylinder (~100 kg). It’s still leagues lighter than a cast-iron V8,
but it adds some extra ounces back over the front wheels.
Length in the Bay: An inline-5 is longer than an inline-4. While a classic
Mustang engine bay has acres of room, an I5 means you have to
measure your ﬁrewall-to-radiator clearance carefully to ensure the
front-mid placement still ﬁts neatly behind the core support without
cuting the front grille.
3. Highway Eﬃciency & The 45 MPG Goal
Can it still hit 45 MPG? Yes, but it requires precise tuning. Because it
has ﬁve cylinders instead of four, internal pumping friction is
marginally higher. However, when cruising on the highway in 6th gear
at 2,150 RPM completely out of boost, a well-calibrated standalone
ECU can lean-burn a Volvo T5 eﬃciently enough to land right around
40 to 43 MPG—dropping just a fraction behind the MZR's peak
eﬃciency.
Is it Viable? (The Real-World Verdict)
Financially and logistically, it is harder, but entirely doable.
The Hurdles: You have to use a custom adapter plate (e.g., matching
the Volvo whiteblock patern to a BMW ZF transmission or a Volvo 960
manual bellhousing), fabricate custom engine mount arms, and build a
custom wiring harness.
The Payoﬀ: You keep your exact 265 HP / 335 Nm reliability threshold,
you keep amazing highway eﬃciency, you gain a broader low-end
torque band, and—most importantly—you get that legendary,
intoxicating 5-cylinder turbo soundtrack echoing out of a classic

10/19/26, 2:00 AM

Mustang shell.

get a unique powerplant and sound, trading the MZR for a Volvo T5 inline-5
turns Project Loki into an absolute icon.

How would the car sound?
With the Mazda 2.0L MZR/Duratec turbo pushing exhaust out through a
custom exhaust system, Project Loki won't sound like a screaming, raspy 4-
cylinder civic, nor will it sound like a traditional pushrod V8. Instead, it will
have a very distinct, modern, high-performance character:
1. At Idle & Low RPM (The Stealth Proﬁle)
At a stoplight, it will have a crisp, slightly deeper idle than a stock
Mazda hatchback because of the custom exhaust diameter and
standalone ECU timing.
With the windows up, it will idle smoothly and quietly—giving away
https://gemini.google.com/share/01e19b0f2d93?social_preview=false&skid=a8e7c356-0494-4c69-b5c1-8f2a48d378be
10/19/26, 2:00 AM

almost no hints of what's hiding under that classic hood until you
touch the throtle.
2. On Acceleration (The Turbo Symphony)
As you roll onto the throtle and the turbo starts spooling at around
2,500 to 3,000 RPM, you’ll get a sharp, aggressive turbo spool whistle
and induction hiss building up right under the dash.
When you shif gears at high RPM (redlining near 7,200 RPM), a well-
tuned recirculating blow-oﬀ valve or bypass valve will give oﬀ a clean,
crisp "pﬀf" or sharp woosh, reminiscent of a modern rally car or
high-end turbo sports coupe.
3. The Exhaust Note
No "Ricey" Drone: Because it’s a high-ﬂowing 2.0L block with a turbo
acting as a natural muﬄer on the exhaust side, you want to avoid a
cheap, hollow muﬄer. Pairing it with a quality straight-through
glasspack or a chambered turbo muﬄer (like a Magnaﬂow or Borla
setup) on a 2.5-inch or 3-inch custom mandrel-bent exhaust will
change everything.
The Result: It produces a deep, throaty, resonant baritone growl
rather than a high-pitched buzz. When you are hard on the throtle
through the mid-range, it screams with a clean, mechanical snarl that
sounds purposeful, sharp, and modern.
4. Cruising on the Highway (The 45 MPG Quiet)
At your sweet spot of 2,150 RPM in 6th gear cruising at 110 km/h, the
engine will be practically whispering.
There will be no annoying drone or cabin resonance to give you a
headache on long trips—just a low, steady hum, allowing you to cruise
comfortably like a proper grand tourer.
It bridges two worlds: classic vintage styling on the outside, but when you
step on it it sounds like a modern precision-engineered turbo track car
